## 4.7 Result

The smart energy meter monitoring system was constructed according to the design. [Figure 4.14](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_014) shows the completed system circuit on the stripboard. The interconnection was performed by soldering the components according to the schematic diagram. The sizing and arrangement of the components on the strip board were designed to fit into the DIN rail enclosure. The entire prototype was completed as shown in [Figure 4.15](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_015). The DB box has the energy meter, circuit breaker and enclosure installed on the DIN rail.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_014.jpg)

Figure 4.14: Completed system circuit on a stripboard.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_015.jpg)

Figure 4.15: The entire completed prototype of the IoT-enabled single-phase energy meter monitoring system.

Functionality validation of the prototype was carried out. The power cord of the prototype was plugged into the residential socket outlet on the wall which rated single-phase voltage level of 240 V[AC] and a laptop was connected to the dedicated socket as a load to draw power. An industrial standard meter was used to compare with the measurement result of the energy meter. Janitza UMG96RM digital power meter was used to verify the accuracy of the DDM18SD energy meter. A laptop was used as the load to be measured by both meters.

[Figure 4.16](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_016) shows the voltage measurements of the meters. Voltage reading at energy meter is 244.3 V which is approximately same as the value of 244 V at the Janitza meter. DDM18SD energy meter displays higher precision at the display screen. [Figure 4.17](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_017) shows the current measurements of the meters. Current readings at the energy meter and Janitza meter are the same which is 0.14 A. [Figure 4.18](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_018) shows the frequency measurement of the meters. Both meters measured 50 Hz of frequency from the power supply. Hence, the accuracy of the energy meter measurement is ensured.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_016.jpg)

Figure 4.16: (a) Voltage reading at DDM18SD energy meter and (b) voltage reading at Janitza digital power meter.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_017.jpg)

Figure 4.17: (a) Current reading at DDM18SD energy meter and (b) current reading at Janitza digital power meter.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_018.jpg)

Figure 4.18: (a) Frequency reading at DDM18SD energy meter and (b) frequency reading at Janitza digital power meter.

WeMos board was connected to the USB port of the laptop to load the system program from Arduino IDE. The process was observed using the serial monitor in Arduino IDE. Baud rate of 9,600kbps was used for the serial communication between WeMos and the laptop. The serial monitor must use the same baud rate to read data correctly. The program starts with configuring parameters of the software serial communication, Modbus communication, Wi-Fi network, NTP client-server at the backend. Then, Wi-Fi connection is established first as per the program flow. [Figure 4.19](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_019) shows WeMos board successfully established Wi-Fi connection at the serial monitor. Then NTP client was initiated and the system will continuously request the time data from the NTP server to check whether the currentseconds value equal to zero indicating the next minute. At a 1-minute interval, data values will be read at the WeMos from the data register of the energy meterusing Modbus-RTU protocol. Lastly, the data is sent to the ThingSpeak channel and the whole process will be kept on repeating. [Figure 4.20](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_020) shows the system reads the electricity values successfully. Serial monitor shows the expected output from the successful run of the system program. The voltage is 244.4 V, the current is 0.14 A, the active power is 16.7 W and the frequency is 50 Hz. The information was subsequently sent to ThingSpeak channel using HTTP POST method.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_019.jpg)

Figure 4.19: Serial monitor shows Wi-Fi connection is established.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_020.jpg)

Figure 4.20: Successful data reading and sending through the ThingSpeak channel at the serial monitor.

Last, the system was run with the load over 24 h to validate its reliability and consistency. Manipulations were made to the load between the period to observe the change in consumption. Data was collected every minute on the ThingSpeak channel, and the final datasets were exported in CSV file format at the end for further analysis purposes. The total number of data points required to collect is 60 min per hour times 24 h per day which is equal to 1,440 data points per day.

The exported data contains the expected number of data pointswhich shows the consistency of the system performance in monitoring energy and sending data to the cloud on a per-minute basis throughout 24 h. [Figures 4.21](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_021) [and 4.22](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_022) show the per-minute voltage level and frequency reading of the power supply over 24 h, respectively. According to Malaysia electricity standard, socket outlets are required to have 240 V[AC] and have tolerance of +10%/–6% within the nominal voltage, where the frequency is 50 Hz and has ±1% of tolerance. The voltage readings were varied. Based on [Figure 4.21](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_021), the average voltage captured in 24 h was 244.94 V[AC] with minimum voltage was 240.7 V[AC] and the maximum voltage was 250 V[AC] within the acceptable range. The frequency reading was kept constant at 50 Hz all the time according to [Figure 4.22](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_022) This proves the accuracy of the system performance to read the correct value.

The varying voltage level could be due to the underlying electricity consumption patterns by the consumers from the grid. The voltage level at midnight till early morning was relatively higher than the other period. This scenario may suggest that overall power taken from the grid was lesser at midnight since most of the people are sleeping. Hence, the voltage level increased during the period and had the highest value recorded at around 250 V[AC]. However, the voltage started to drop from 7 am to 9.30 am in the morning when people started to use more electrical appliances to prepare for the day. Then the voltage increases again but still lower than that at midnight. The voltage level is relatively stable in the afternoon between 2 pm and 6 pm since people were using electricity constantly to do work. When the time reaches 7 pm, people are at home and use electrical appliances for cooking, entertainment, air-conditioning and other purposes. Hence, voltage is slightly lower between 7 pm and 10 pm with the lowest value recorded of 240.7 V[AC].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_021.png)

Figure 4.21: Per-minute voltage level of the power supply over 24 h.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_022.png)

Figure 4.22: Per-minute frequency reading of the power supply over 24 h.

[Figure 4.23](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_023) shows the per-minute current drawn by the laptop over 24 h. The laptop was kept on running over 24 h and it was charging at fully charged condition. Between 12 am and 11 am, current readings were relatively lower at around 0.06 A because it was not used by the user during the period hence the laptop went sleeping mode during that period. After 11 am in the morning, current drawn by the laptop increased to around 0.09 A because the user started to use the laptop to do work. The overall current drawn was constant and stable except for the readings at around 1 pm. A sudden drop at around 1 pm can be noticed which was because the laptop was unplugged from the outlet for a few minutes and used the battery to power itself. Then a sudden rise of the current reading of 0.47 A was recorded when the laptop was plugged to the socket and draw power again. This was because the battery level was lower than the originally full charged condition, hence it draws more current to charge the battery while powering up the laptop.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_023.png)

Figure 4.23: Per-minute current drawn by the laptop over 24 h.

[Figure 4.24](#9783110713794_010746.xhtml_b_9783110714043-004_fig_024) shows the per-minute active power consumed by the laptop over 24 h. It shows the same trend as in the “current versus time” graph. Maximum active power of 101.9 W was recorded at 1 pm when the laptop battery level was not fully charged. The active power was stable during the rest of the time. The values were at around 5 W before 10:30 am and at around 8 W after that. In between 12 am and 10:30 am, the active power intermittently drops to zero because the laptop in sleeping mode consumes little power and it stops drawing power when fully charged. On the other side, after 10:30 am, active power did not drop to zero because the user was starting to use it and run with programs hence drawing more power for the operation. Therefore, the usage behavior of the user over 24 h can be interpreted from the results.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-004_fig_024.png)

Figure 4.24: Per-minute active power consumed by the laptop over 24 h.

## 4.8 Conclusion

In conclusion, this book chapter provides detailed information and concept to build a low-cost smart meter energy monitoring system. The prototype was constructed using devices compliant with the international standard which provides reliable performance and safety. The software implementation of the energy monitoring system including the configuration of system program and IoT platform was described in detail. The system design can be implemented easily using any energy monitoring device adopting Modbus-RTU communication protocol and RS-485 interface. Existing systems can be upgraded into a smart IoT system by referring to this book chapter. The smart metering data can be used for analysis to obtain the anticipated value of a smart meter. Hence, the future development plans include load profiling analysis and enhancements of the system features. Load profiling analysis can be performed by collecting sample power consumption data from certain sectors to gain insight into the consumers’ energy usage patterns. The data can be used to project the expected currentbilling and usage for the consumers to plan their energy usage. Local storage can be implemented with a microSD card and the adapter module to ensure data integrity. The alert function can be added into the system program to send a reminder to the consumers when exceeding the consumption threshold set by the users. In this book chapter, power information of a laptop is monitored and recorded using the system and data trend can be interpreted to understand the usage profile at any minute. User can view visualization of the information remotely and adjust the usage accordingly. Apart from that, this system can be implemented to monitor all other appliance and devices as well as can be easily scale up using the Modbus RS-485 multiple point systems. All other commodities can be connected and monitored to establish a network that enables machine-to-machine communication between monitoring devices. Abundance of information can be collected from the network which is very useful for analysis and decision making especially when integrated with control system. As the technology advances, people will have more facilities, appliances or devices to fulfill convenience and leisure in life, and a complete energy managementand control system will become important not only in industry level, but in every home.

# 5 Internet of things (IoT)-based smart grids

Monika YadavUniversity of Petroleum and Energy Studies, Dehradun, IndiaAmritansh MehrotraUniversity of Petroleum and Energy Studies, Dehradun, IndiaDevender Kumar SainiUniversity of Petroleum and Energy Studies, Dehradun, India

#### Abstract

The current grid has only one-way communication system with the load which can be termed as a monologue. This does not allow the grid to communicate with the load side to manage itself. When there is a way communication between the two then we can call it as a dialogue. This makes an ordinary grid “*smart grid*.” The smart grid represents an unprecedented opportunity to move the energy industry into a new era of reliability, availability, and efficiency that will contribute to our economic and environmental health.

To make a grid smart, new sensors and measurement instrumentations will have to be added in the system. These instruments will take real-time data from the grid as well as the demand side which will be then used for proper monitoring and planning. To collect and analyze this real-time data will be stored in cloud and can be made accessible anywhere. For this purpose, Internet of things (IoT) will prove to be very useful. In this chapter we will start from the basics of smart grid and IoT, giving information about each parameter and instruments that we can use in IoT and smart grid. Then we will show some examples from the present-day world where this is used. We will also discuss the advantages and disadvantages of this method.

Keywords: Internet of things (IoT), smart grid, application of IoT in smart grid,

## 5.1 Introduction

A grid can be understood as a system of interconnected networks which is used to deliver electricity from the producers to the consumers [[1](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_001), [2](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_002), [3](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_003)]. It is also known as power grid, electrical grid, or electric grid [[4](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_004), [5](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_005), [6](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_006)]. Parts of an electrical grid are listed below:

1.  Generating stations: Generating stations are the point on the grid where electricity is generated by the power plants. The generation can be done by using thermal power plants, hydroelectric power plant, nuclear, solar, wind, and so on [[7](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_007), [8](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_008), [9](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_009), [10](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_010)].

2.  Electrical substation: These stations are used for stepping up the voltage for transmission, or stepping down for distribution [[11](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_011), [12](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_012)].

3.  High-voltage transmission lines: These transmission lines are used to carry the power from generation place to distributions place. They should have proper insulations so that they can carry high voltages [[13](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_013), [14](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_014)].

4.  Low-voltage distribution lines: These are used to connect individual costumers.

The current grid has only one-way communication system with the load which can be termed as a monologue. This doesn’t allow the grid to communicate with the load. But if the grid can communicate with the load side it can be very beneficial for both generating side and consumer side.

When there is a two-way communication for the enhancement of reliability and sustainability between the load side and the generation side then we term that as a dialogue [[1](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_001)]. The grid is called “*smart grid*.” Now we shall further understand what a smart grid really is and how can an ordinary grid be made smart.

### 5.1.1 What is a smart grid?

A smart grid is a system which can communicate with the load side itself so that it can understand what the load requirements are. The smart grid consists of a variety of operation and energy measures including smart meters, modern appliances, renewable and energy efficient resources. Smart grid will be a type of system in which user safety will be ensured by continuous monitoring and updating from the grid. This can be done by using smart meters and monitoring systems in the grid to ensure the electronic communication between the load and grid [[15](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_015)]. In paper [[16](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_016)], various communication methods are defined with respect to distance. An important outcome targeted in smart grid is to transfer the high-quality energy to consumers [[17](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_017)]. The communication infrastructure for smart grid can be visualized as shown in [Figure 5.1](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_001) [[18](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_018)]. In later sections, various communication technologies that are currently being used in smart grid are discussed.

The smart grid will allow consumer participation, resistance to attacks, optimal power delivery and better power factors [[15](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_015)], although grid code protocols have to be followed for any changes in the power system networking [[19](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_019)]. This all can be done by deep involvement of information technology. The components that are used and how they are made to work in proper synchronization are discussed in further sections.

It is evident from Figure 5.1, that control system plays a vital role between generation side and consumer side for sharing the information. This is done so that in case of any fault or overloading the generating stations or control system can take proper actions which will help in continuous supply of electricity.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_001.jpg)

Figure 5.1: Communication infrastructure.

### 5.1.2 Structure of conventional grid

In [Figure 5.2](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_002) a classical electrical grid can be visualized. In the classical electrical grid, there is one-way communication. One component of the grid cannot communicate with other components.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_002.jpg)

Figure 5.2: Information sharing [[7](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_007)].

[Figure 5.3](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_003) illustrates centralized, decentralized, and distributed topology of electrical energy production [[15](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_015)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_003.jpg)

Figure 5.3: Classical electrical grid.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_004.jpg)

Figure 5.4: Energy generation distributions [45].

These different energy generation distributions are discussed as follows [[21](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_021)]:

1.  Centralized: This refers to a large-scale generation of electricity at centralized facilities. These facilities are generally located away from the customers because of safety measures. They are connected to the customers with the help of high-tension transmission wires which can handle high voltages. These generation units include fossil fuel–based thermal power plant, hydroelectric power plant, etc.

2.  Decentralized: In this the generation load is not on just one generation unit but is slightly distributed. They can be small-scale energy generators which can provide power to nearby town or area. They may be stand-alone or can be connected with other generating stations as required. These are mainly based on approach of distributed energy generation distribution systems.

3.  Distributed:This is referred to as small-scale generation unit which are located on or near the location of use. The case that users can be producers can also be included in this. Again, these can be stand-alone or connected to each other by a network which can be used to share power generated. In later stages, they become locally distributed energy network systems.

There are three main reasons why a classical grid should be converted to a smart grid:

1.  Increase in energy demands:

    *   The introduction of new technologies can create a very high demand for energy in the near future. One example is the introduction of electric vehicles which can create a burden on classical grid.

2.  To decrease illegal usage and losses:

    *   In many places around the world there is illegal usage. By the help of smart grid these can be stopped and losses can also be reduced.

    *   Illegal wire tappings that are done in various places in India. Because of these power companies have to face losses as well as the stability of the grid is also compromised.

3.  Increase the production and carriage capacity of existing plants.

### 5.1.3 System requirements for communication system

The communication network between energy generation, transmission, distribution, and consumption requires a two-way communication. This means that all the substations as well as consumers should be able to contact one other anytime. The two-way communication system plays a very vital role in smart grids.

These systems should not only be fast but they should also be secure and reliable so that the chances of errors are reduced in the system.

Major requirements are as follows [[18](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_018)]:

1.  Security: Some information is very important for billing purpose as well as grid control. This information should be stored in a secured place. Information security is also very important to defend the cyberattacks that can happen anytime.

2.  Reliability: The system should be reliable. It should be robust in nature. These have become some of the most prioritized system requirements at this time. There are many reasons why a system can become unreliable: load on system, old power infrastructure, and so on [[22](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_022)]. To achieve this both wireless systems with proper bandwidth and security and wired technologies with increased capacity, security and reliability can be used. If required a hybrid of these systems can also be used [[23](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_023)].

3.  Scalability: A smart grid should be scalable enough to facilitate addition of new components. It should be able to handle advanced web services as well as addition of new meters, data collectors, and new renewable energy resources. They should be secure and self-configuring in nature [[24](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_024)].

4.  Quality of service: The communication between generation and consumer is the key issue with smart grid. Performance degradation can occur due to any reason but this will affect the stability of the system, so we need systems which can avoid these difficulties and at the same time they should satisfy the communication requirements [46].

### 5.1.4 Communication technologies available

At present there are many communication technologies available; some of them are as listed:

1.  ZigBee

2.  Wireless mesh

3.  Cellular network communication

4.  Powerline communication

5.  Digital subscriber lines (DSL)

### 5.1.5 Introduction to Internet of things (IoT)

Internet of things (IoT) is an information-based network which connects various elements and objects of a system with one another with the help of advanced information and communication technologies (ICT) as well as advanced embedded systems which consists of sophisticated and digitalized sensors, meters, and controllers [[25](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_025)].

IoT is also considered as a third revolution in the realms of information technologies. Internet of energy (IoE) presents an upgraded version of IoT which deals with the combination of advanced information technologies and energy ecosystems.

Presence of IoT in smart grid enhances the ability of each component to a grid to share information through a wired or wireless network. IoT is regarded as an indispensable part of the implementation of smart grids and setting up smart cities and smart building schemes [[26](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_026)].

In the presence of a large number and diverse devices, equipment, and energy form inherit their behaviors, reliability, and variability for some parameters in energy fields. This can produce unpredictable or chaotic nature for some phenomenon. To handle such conditions, large amounts of data should be collected and analyzed in near real time so that decisions can be taken with a brief time delay. This data should be quickly and securely shared with the destination and processes must start immediately. IoT-based devices are those that equipped with electric boards encompassing microcontrollers and microprocessors with the ability to share information which can be sensors, meters, or controllers.

All this has to be done while ensuring the safety of data and the digital systems from cyberattacks or unintentional interruptions (disturbances).

For proper development of any IoT-based project the following technologies have to be used [[14](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_014)]:

1.  Radio frequency identification (RFID)

2.  Wireless sensor networks (WSN)

3.  Middleware

4.  Cloud computing

5.  IoT application software

### 5.1.6 Evolution of IoT

Various types of IoT applications have emerged. [Table 5.2](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_tab_002) shows the evolution of IoT technologies like network, software and algorithm, hardware and data processing.

| Parameters | Before 2010 | 2010–2015 | 2015–2020 | Beyond 2020 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Network | Sensor network | Self-aware and self-organizing networks Sensor network location transparency
Storage networks and power networks
Hybrid networking technologies | Network context awareness | Network cognition Self-learning, self-repairing networks |
| Software and algorithms | Relational database integration IoT-oriented RDBMS
Event-based platforms
Sensor middleware
Sensor networks middleware
Proximity/localization algorithms | Large-scale, open semantic software modules Composable algorithms
Next-generation IoT-based social software
Next-generation IoT-based enterprise applications | Goal-oriented software Distributed intelligence, problem solving
Things-to-things collaboration environments | User-oriented software The invisible IoT
Easy-to-deploy IoT software
Things-to-humans collaboration |
| Hardware | RFID tags and some sensors Sensors built into mobile devices
NFC in mobile phones

Secure, low-cost tags (e.g., Silent Tags) | Smart sensors (biochemical) More sensors and actuators (tiny sensors) | Nanotechnology and new materials |
| Data Processing | Serial data processing Parallel data processing | Energy, frequency spectrum-aware data processing Data processing context adaptable | Context-aware data processing and data responses | Cognitive processing and optimization |

## 5.2 IoT in smart grid networks

In a smart grid, a wider range of equipment will be under control which will ensure more pliability for power grids. This means that in a grid many of the components can be controlled by the grid operator. To ensure proper controlling of a large number as well as a variety of equipment, the smartness of the grid should be enhanced manifold. This can be done only by using big data analytic technologies.

The technical requirements for rejuvenation of power system are divided in four layers [[25](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_025), [28](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_028)]:

1.  Data analysis layer: This layer consists of data visualization, advanced metering infrastructure (AMI)-provided data analysis, equipment’s data analysis.

2.  Application layer: Data management and acquisition and equipment’s supervision are included in this layer [[27](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_027)].

3.  Communication and control layer: This layer includes SCADA systems, Internet-protocol-aided networks, bidirectional wired or wireless network like Wi-Fi, GPRS, and PLC’s HomePlug.

4.  Physical equipment in grid edge layer: This includes supervising and monitoring equipment, storage, voltage control equipment, protection, modern sensors, and switches for the power grid [[28](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_028)].

### 5.2.1 Application of IoT in smart grid at different levels of power system

IoT can be used at different levels of a grid. The levels include:

1.  Generation level: In this level the generation of electricity takes place. This is done by different power plants.

2.  Transmission level: In this level, the generated power is transmitted from power stations to distribution stations. For this the voltage is stepped up and the power is fed to the high-tension transmission lines.

3.  Distribution level: In this level, the power received at the distribution stations is further supplied to consumers with the help of transmission lines.

[Figure 5.5](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_005) gives an idea of paradigm of IoT in smart grids. Application in each level is discussed.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_005.jpg)

Figure 5.5: Different paradigms of IoT in electrical smart grid.

### 5.2.2 IoT for generation sector

Without IoT in generation sector the power stations can face financial losses due to electricity losses. This can also cause instability in the grid. They may not be able to provide sufficient power required to meet load demands. Even if load demands are met it will not be economical to consumers. So IoT is required in the generation sector.

[Figure 5.6](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_006) shows the application of IoT in generation level. In this level it is used for real-time data collection from every generating station linked to the grid. It can be used to gather the information about generation efficiency as well as generation cost for each plant. Until now the IoT was only focused on the demand side with less concern paid to generation side. Applying this method to this level can unlock further stages of increased efficiency and performance.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_006.jpg)

Figure 5.6: Application of IoT in generation level for real-time analysis.

In this also IoT can be used in different renewable generation methods like:

1.  IoT in wind power plants:The wind power plants are changing more rapidly in terms of scale as well as efficiency. Many energy experts see wind and solar new targets of developments in the near future. In addition, better collaborative operation with energy storage facilities can be maintained if and only if real-time data exchange is available between wind units and energy storage units embedded in the system [[29](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_029)].

2.  IoT in solar power plants: After the first and second industrial revolutions, the exploitation of nonrenewable energy resources got more attention. Global warming and climate change have also made people adopt these new methods. In solar energy the monitoring of module efficiency and array performance is the key task that IoT performs. It also provides stability to the grid when a solar plant is connected with the active grid.

### 5.2.3 IoT for transmission sector

Transmission level is present between the generation and distribution levels. This level holds a very important role in a power system as it ensures reliable power supply to the demand side. IoT-equipped intelligent devices can be installed in the transmission level which can inform operators about the changes in the electrical supply or disturbances in the system.

The monitoring of real-time power flowing through the lines enables the operator to manage congestion automatically in congested power systems or locally congested areas, particularly in emergencies and contingencies. Hence, the maneuverability of the operator can be increased [[30](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_030)].

The IoT devices can be embedded in transmission grid which are sync node device, tower deviation sensor, meteorological sensors (temperature and humidity sensors), conductor acceleration sensor, wind speed sensor, current leakage sensor, and sag sensors. These devices can help to have enhanced real-time monitoring on the conductor, insulations and towers.

### 5.2.4 IoT for distribution sector

In order to make a smart grid, all the critical points of the distribution network should be equipped with IoT. Installation of AMIs is the first and foremost thing that should be done. These systems provide a bidirectional, consistent, and secure connection between servers and data collectors, consumers, and so on.

In order to improve the reliability of the grid, self-healing is a vital scheme of the grid. Self-healing should occur in real time to ensure appropriate and desired functionality of the grid as quickly as possible after enduring a fault.

Different areas in distribution level where IoT can be used are:

1.  Active distribution networks

2.  Microgrids

3.  Smart cities, buildings, and homes

4.  Industrial IoT (IIoT)

[Figure 5.7](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_007) shows integration of IoT in distribution level [[25](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_025)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_007.jpg)

Figure 5.7: Integration of IoT in distribution level [[25](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_025)].

## 5.3 Metering system

This section describes the design and development of smart energy meter. The readings are captured from sensors like voltage, current, and PF sensors. The data packet is transferred to NodeMCUand the NodeMCU sends data on closed servers.

In current meter systems the meter readers have to travel a lot to get readings of a large number of meters every day. In order to do so they have to reach location of every customer to record the readings. This can increase a chance of error due to humans as they have to handle a very large set of data. Automatic meter reading system (AMR) can come very handy here as they record the meter reading and status of meters and then send this data to centralized unit for billing. Further, IoT eases up the whole network procedure by providing simple and secure two-way communication between utility and consumer as shown in [Figure 5.8](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_008).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_008.jpg)

Figure 5.8: Overview of components for AMI [[31](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_031)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-005_fig_009.jpg)

Figure 5.9: Block diagram of smart energy meter [[32](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_032)].

In [Figure 5.9](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_fig_009), block diagram of smart energy meters can be visualized. The microcontroller in this plays a very important role as all the processing is done by it. Wi-Fi is used to upload and download the data from the cloud. Cloud storages are also very important as some of the information is vital for the system like billing. An LCD is required to display certain values, for example, energy used [[32](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_032)].

## 5.4 Real-world examples

Many European nations [[33](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_033), [34](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_034)] as well as Asian countries are planning to use IoT to make their grid smart.

Detailed studies for requirements in countries like China [[20](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_020)] and Taiwan [[35](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_035)] have been done. Their work includes study of all the parameters that can affect the smart grid and methods or infrastructure they wish to apply to overcome those problems.

Developed countries like the United States [[34](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_034)] and European countries are looking forward in setting up these smart grids. In the near future, it will be a necessity for every country to have smart grids. Then only they can cope up with increasing energy demands as well as changes that are yet to come in energy sector like introduction of electric vehicles and V2G technologies [[36](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_036), [37](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_037), [38](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_038), [39](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_039), [40](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_040), [41](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_041), [42](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_042), [43](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_043), [44](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_044)].

Countries like India first have to deeply analyze all the factors that can cause faults or failures in these types of grid. Only after proper analysis can they think of making such grids. The main problem these countries face is high population as well as high energy demands [[47-51](#9783110713794_012982.xhtml_b_9783110714043-005_ref_032)].

### 5.4.1 Advantages and disadvantages

Advantages:

*   Better customer services

*   Better billing and accurate systems

*   Fraud detection

*   Less technical losses

*   Better load sharing

*   Reduction in carbon emissions

Disadvantages:

*   Continuous communication should be available always; then only the system can work properly.

*   During emergency conditions, network congestions and performance can be pose a big problem to the system.

*   As there is no guarantee of continuous supply of Internet by providers during adverse conditions, the system becomes less reliable during these conditions.

*   Threat of cyberattacks is always there.

*   The installation cost of these advanced devices is very high and, in some cases, maintenance cost is also high.

To overcome all these disadvantages, proper R&D should be done on smart grid by using IoT. The problems found in the system are not nonremovable. Proper planning should be done to make the system free of any threats and failures.
