# 6 Maximum power point tracking control under partial shading conditions using particle swarm optimization algorithm

Jia Shun KohFaculty of Engineering, Technology and Built Environment, UCSI University Kuala LumpurRodney H. G. TanFaculty of Engineering, Technology and Built Environment, UCSI University Kuala LumpurWei Hong LimFaculty of Engineering, Technology and Built Environment, UCSI University Kuala Lumpur

#### Abstract

This chapter presents maximum power point tracking (MPPT) control using particle swarm optimization (PSO) algorithm for photovoltaic (PV) panel affected by partial shading due to shadow casting. Partial shading casted on a PV panel will produce multiples peaks power characteristic curve; thus, tracking the global peak becomes a challenge especially under dynamic changing partial shading condition. The MPPT PSO used DC–DC buck converter as energy conversion medium to deliver the tracked power to the load. PSO algorithm tracks the MPP by reading the PV panel output power and changing the PWM duty cycle of the buck converter for the PV panel to operate at the maximum power point. The MPPT PSO is implemented in MATLAB/Simulink platform, the model includes a PV Panel with three bypass diodes, power electronics DC–DC buck converter circuitry connected to a load resistor and the PSO algorithm is coded in an embedded function block. The criteria of convergent and reinitialization of PSO algorithm are discussed. The maximum power tracking performance is evaluated on different set of PSO parameters including the population size, acceleration coefficients and inertia weight. The results from each sets of parameters are discussed, and it is reported that the populations size of 4, inertia weight of 0.1, as well as acceleration coefficients of *c*[1] = 0.8 and *c*[2] = 1.2 are the optimal parameter settings of PSO used in this study. Last, the MPPT PSO is also evaluated with dynamic changing partial shading conditions to evaluate its tracking robustness. The best average tracking time and tracking error recorded by the proposed method are 0.06 s and 0.15%, respectively, implying its feasibility for real time application. This chapter provides a comprehensive insight on how PSO parameters affect the performance of MPPT in terms of tracking time, accuracy and stability for practical tracking application.

Keywords: particle swarm optimization, maximum power point tracking, buck converter, partial shading,

## 6.1 Introduction

In 1839, Antione-Cesar Becquerel found that ray from the Sun striking on specific materials could generate electricity. Fifty-years later, Charles Fritts invented the first photovoltaic (PV) cells. In 1883, first PV panels consist of multiple solar cells was installed on New York City rooftop. Since then, huge efforts have been done by researchers in improving the efficiency of the PV panel. PV panel efficiency can be affected by its materials and operating power points. The characteristic of the PV panel is indicated by two curves, I–V characteristic curve and P–V curve, the knee of I–V curve and crest of the P–V curve is referred to as the maximum power point (MPP) [[1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_001), [2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_002), [3](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_003), [4](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_004)]. Several PV panels are assembled in series and parallel to construct a PV array in order to produce the required voltage and current. In 1969, Blake and Hanson observed the hotspot failure on PV arrays in the space and recommend the utilization of bypass diodes to solve the problem [[1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_001)]. When PV panels are partially shaded by shadow cast by nearby buildings or tree, the power generation at shaded section will be reduced significantly and serve as load. Due to the small resistance of shaded solar cells group, large current generated by other cells passing through it may heat up the solar cells and cause irreversible damage. Hence, the bypass diodes need to be connected to redirect the current away from the shaded solar cells when it’s current is higher than certain threshold values. Although the utilization of bypass diode has solved the hot spot problem, this method creates another challenge in PV power characteristic curve known as multiple peaks which consist of local and global peaks as shown in [Figure 6.1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_001). The lower peak is called local maximum power point (LMPP) and the highest peak is called global maximum power point (GMPP) [[5](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_005), [6](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_006), [7](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_007), [8](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_008), [9](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_009), [10](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_010)].The maximum possible numbers of peaks present in PV power characteristic curve is equal to the count of bypass diodes used, where the PV power characteristic curve with two peaks are commonly encountered in partial shading conditions [[11](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_011), [12](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_012), [13](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_013)]. The recent study of Teo et al. [[31](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_031)] in 2018 explains the effect of partial shading with different shading heaviness and number of modules through an informative diagram. The operation principle of the bypass diode in a typical standard solar PV panel consists of three groups of PV cell are illustrated in [Figure 6.1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_001). One group of PV cell that is not shaded is exposed to a solar irradiance of 764 W/m² is able to generate 6.685 A of current, whereas the other two groups of PV cell are shaded with solar irradiance of 208 W/m² generating 1.82 A of current because it is not able to generate sufficiently high current to satisfy the load demand. The application of bypass diode produces staircase-like I–V current curve and P–V power curve with multiple peaks [[14](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_014)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_001.png)

Figure 6.1: PV panel with two shaded PV cell groups.

Similar observation can be found in [Figure 6.2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002). The two PV cell groups that are not shaded with solar irradiance of 886 W/m² can generate the current of 7.7 A, while the shaded PV cell group with solar irradiance of 241 W/m² can generate the current of 2.109 A. The latter PV cell group will be bypassed when the load is drawing more than 2.109 A of current.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_002.png)

Figure 6.2: PV panel with one shaded PV cell groups.

From [Figures 6.1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_001) and [6.2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002), it is observed that the global peak can be located at the lower voltage represent on the left side peak or at the higher voltage represent on the right side peak of the PV power curve depended on the shaded PV cell groups. These two sets of P–V power curve will be used to evaluate the performance of the MPPT PSO in [Section 6.4](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_s_006_s_004).

Extensive review has been done by Karami et al. [[22](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_022)] on different MPPT techniques, tracking approach with invariable parameters, magnitude from sensors, experimentation, mathematical formula and AI forecasting. According to El-Khozondar et al. [17], Perturb and Observe (P&O), hill-climbing (HC), incremental conductance(IC) algorithms are broadly adopted MPPT algorithm [[16](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_016), [17](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_017)]. P&O and HC are famous because of the simple, economical and uncomplicated implementation. These two methods are not suitable for tracking the rapid changing irradiance due to their slow convergence speed. IC can track under rapid changing irradiance with higher tracking efficiency; however, the algorithm is computational expensive. Furthermore, all these methods suffered from the problem on trapping at local peak for multi peak curve caused by partial shading.

Particle swarm optimization (PSO) is nature-inspired algorithms broadly applied to solve MPPT tracking problem due to its straightforward implementation and economical computation cost characteristic. PSO is considered as an intelligent optimization algorithm proposed by Eberhart and Kennedy [15] predicated on the social behaviors of bird flocking in seeking for food [[15](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_015)]. PSO is a population-based stochastic algorithm consists of multiple particles with different velocity, position, and fitness values. Each PSO particle is investigated as a promising resolution to solve an optimization problem, such as MPPT tracking in this chapter. During the optimization process, each particle can perform searching in solution space by adjusting its velocity based on its personal best position and the global best position. For each *i*th particle, the corresponding velocity *v*[*i*]^(*k*) and position *x*[*i*]^(*k*) in every (*k* + 1)th iteration can be updated by using the following equations:

(6.1)**v**[**i**]^(**k** + 1) = **w****v**[**i**]^(**k**) + **c**[1] * **r**[1](**P**[**b****e****s****t****i**] − **x**[**i**]^(**k**)) + **c**[2] * **r**[2](**G**[**b****e****s****t**] − **x**[**i**]^(**k**))(6.2)**x**[**i**]^(**k** + 1) = **x**[**i**]^(**k**) + *v*[**i**]^(**k** + 1)

where *c*[1] and *c*[2] are the acceleration coefficient, set in the range between 0.1 and 2, *w* is the inertia weight to indicate the influence of particle’s previous velocity, and it is set between 0 and 1\. *r*[1] and *r*[2] are two random numbers generated from uniform distributions with values between 0 and 1\. *P*[best] is the local best position memorized by the *i*th particle and *G*[best] is the global best position. In the context of using PSO to resolve the MPPT tracking issue with DC–DC buck converter topology, the relationship between each PSO parameter and corresponding power electronic parameters can be summarized in [Table 6.1](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_tab_001).

Table 6.1:The relationship between PSO and power electronics parameters.

| PSO parameter | Power electronic parameter |
| --- | --- |
| Current positions (*x*[*i*]^(*k*)) | PWM duty cycle |
| Current velocity (*v*[*i*]^(*k*)) | Change in PWM duty cycle |
| Fitness value | Power generated |
|  *P**e**r**s**o**n**a**l* *b**e**s**t* *p**o**s**i**t**i**o**n*(*P*[*b**e**s**t**i*]) | Best duty cycle found by individual particle |
| *G**l**o**b**a**l* *b**e**s**t* *p**o**s**i**t**i**o**n*(*G*[*b**e**s**t* ]) | Best duty cycle found by the best particle |

The velocity and position of all PSO particles are first randomly initialized. For individual particle, the velocity, and position can be updated in every iteration based on equation (6.1 and (6.2)), respectively. The rapid convergence characteristic of PSO enables the population to search for the best duty cycle in a very short time in order to control the converter operating at MPPT mode.

## 6.2 Literature review

Since the last decade, PSO is becoming among the most favored approach in solving the maximum power point tracking (MPPT) problems in solar PV research community, because of its benefits of fast convergence and simplicity in implementation [[18](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_018), [19](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_019), [20](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_020), [21](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_021), [22](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_022), [23](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_023), [24](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_024), [25](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_025), [26](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_026), [27](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_027), [28](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_028), [29](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_029), [30](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_030), [31](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_031), [32](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_032)].

A parameter tuning method based on PSO has been proposed by Kamejima et al. to achieve MPP at various partial shading conditions [[21](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_021)]. The parameters of PSO were initialized as *w *= 0.4, *c*[1]= 0.8 and *c*[2]= 1.2, and these parameters were shifted with a step size of 0.2 initially to analyze the optimality. In the later stage, fine-tuning with 0.05 shifting is then applied to achieve the optimal coefficients. The final computed coefficients are *w *= 0.3, *c*[1]= 1.05, *c*[2]= 0.9\. Furthermore, a repulsive term was also introduced to diversify the particles when they are too close with each other in order to prevent their entrapment in local peak regions, especially when low irradiance conditions were encountered.

Ishaque et al. used a conventional PSO with parameters setting of *w *= 0.4, *c*[1]= 1.2 and *c*[2]= 1.6 to quickly track the global peak efficiency [[20](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_020)]. After the convergence criteria was met, the duty cycle was increased and decrease linearly based on the power change in order to track the global peak continuously.

In 2015, Sudhakar Babu et al. proposed modified PSO where the initial positions of particles are calculated [[30](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_030)]. The PSO used *w* = 0.4, *c*[1] = 1.6, *c*[2] = 1.8 was able to track global peak accurately with zero steady state oscillation. The author applies only three computed particles into the PSO algorithm, comparing to conventional PSO where particles are randomly initialized where it will trap at local peak. However, it still poses problem where *w*, *c*[1], and *c*[2] may not be the optimum value.

Zheng et al. declared the PSO parameters as *w *= 0.85, *c*[1]= 2, *c*[2]= 1 to avoid trapping in the local MPP and improve its global search capability [[32](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_032)]. In the subsequent stage of tracking process, the parameter settings of *w *= 0.6, *c*[1]= 1, *c*[2]= 2 were tuned to achieve better local search capability with the aim of accelerating the convergence rate of the system. Chao et al. proposed a multicore PSO operation to locate the GMPP under varying irradiance [[14](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_014)]. Multicore PSO split the PV systems into several groups where each group is handled by one PSO operations. Simulation results show that the multicore PSO operation can maximize the tracking accuracy to around 96% and reduce the tracking time to around 1.3s of MPPT system with multiple connections.

Liu et al. combined PSO together with incremental conductance (INC) method to efficiently track GMPP [[24](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_024)]. INC method was used to track the GMPP when the irradiance is stable, while the PSO with population size of 20, *w *= 0.8 and *c*[1]= *c*[2]= 1.4 was used to rapidly locate the GMPP if there were changes of irradiance. Simulation results showed that the MPPT efficiency is greatly improved by hybridizing both PSO and INC. Grzesiak et al. employed a PSO with five particles to solve MPPT problem [[19](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_019)]. Except for the best particles that was updated with HC technique. The velocity of remaining particles was updated using equation (6.1) with parameters setting of *w *= 0.73 and *c*[1]= *c*[2]= 1.4965 in order to ensure the algorithm can locate the global peak without experiencing premature convergence issue.

Efendi et al. apply MPPT PSO with parameter *w* = 0.5 and *c*[1] = *c*[2] = 0.8 is implemented with Sepic converter to solve partial shading on DC microgrid system [[16](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_016)]. Sepic converter with similar characteristic as buck-boost converter could sweep through the whole PV curve. Nonetheless, Sepic converter has pulsating current, series capacitor with high capacitance and large current handling capability is required, increasing the overall cost.

Mao et al. used PSO with *c*[1]*, c*[2] equals 0.6, 0.8, and a self-adapting weight with maximum 0.9 and minimum 0.4 in their research [[25](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_025)]. According to Mao et al., large initial weight promotes the exploration search and increases the convergence speed approaching the favorable zone of search space. The weight is decreased every iteration until *w*[*m**i**n*] for local refinement. This time-varying inertia weight adjusting method is used to optimize the global and local searches of the algorithm.

Koad et al. have proposed the application of mathematical approach – Lagrange Interpolation (LI) in PSO to assist the searching the global peak within 2 ms and 99.94% efficiency [[23](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_023)]. According to the author, the location of MPPT will be located between 0.7 and 0.92 of *V*[oc]. LI is deployed to estimate the duty cycle of converter at MPP and the approximate the location of GMPP which will be used as initial particle for PSO algorithm and significantly shorten the time to reach GMPP. PSO parameters used are *w* = 0.4, *c*[1] = 0.8 and *c*[2] = 1.2\. Compared to original PSO, LI-PSO is only tracking the point near 0.7–0.92 of *V*[oc] located at the right side of PV curve, under certain partial shading condition (PSC) where the location of peak is located at the left side of PV curve, the algorithm may fail.

Eltamaly et al. highlighted the drawback of PSO algorithm in terms of the challenges to determine the initial values of particles as well as its difficulty to track the sudden change of shading pattern due to the frequent reinitialization process [[18](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_018)]. PSO was also reported to stop converging when it met the convergence criteria and the small changes of irradiance will not be able to trigger its reinitialization process, leading to the slipping of particles from GMPP. Eltamaly et al. suggested an innovative initialization technique for PSO in order to address two limitation of conventional random initialization: that is, (a) slower convergence speed due to the reinitialization of particles at location far from global peak and (b) trapping of particles in local peak due to random element of velocity that lead to the missing of global peak. The scanning PSO (SPSO) used a new equation to calculate the approximate locations of global peak. This equation allows at least one of the particles being initialized near to global peak in order to prevent premature convergence. SPSO has significantly reduced the reinitialization and tracking time of new GMPP to within 0.4 s only.

Ram et al. proposed the hybrid methods in MPPT tracking, PSO and P&O. The authors mentioned that P&O is still the best suited approach as it can guarantee operation at maximum power if initialized correctly [[28](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_028)]. PSO could reach rough position of MPP, switching to P&O with step-size greatly reduced to only 2% producing less oscillation at steady-state. The authors used adaptive weightage with *w*[max] = 1, *w*[min] = 0.3, and other parameters *n* = 5, *c*[1] = 1.4, *c*[2] = 1.8 in PSO.

Referring to the summary of literates related to PSO-based MPPT, it appears that different MPPT PSO techniques possess their particular advantages and disadvantages. It is also observed that most of the papers did not elaborate much on the parameter setting of PSO., some papers referred to other literatures settings and some did not even mention their parameters settings. It is vital to comprehend the behavior and effect of each of the PSO parameters in the context of MPPT application. Both tracking time and tracking error were also not discussed in most previously published works although these two performance indices are important to justify the feasibility of real-time application. Therefore, this chapter presents the insight on how PSO parameters including the number of particles, acceleration and inertia affect the performance of MPPT tracking, in order to provide a better understanding to determine the best combinations of PSO parameters for MPPT application.

## 6.3 Methodology

The MPPT PSO system is implemented using MATLAB/Simulink as presented in [Figure 6.3](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_003). It includes a PV panel block, power electronic DC converter circuitry connected to a load resistor, the PSO algorithm block, and a scope to monitor the tracking performance in terms of output voltage, power to the load, and duty cycle to the buck converter. Different irradiance inputs are provided to PV panels to emulate the partial shading conditions that can lead to PV power curve with multiple peaks. The MPPT PSO tracking algorithm distributes its particles along the search space of the PV power curve to track the GMPP. Based on the PV panel current and voltage input, the MPPT PSO algorithm can compute the fitness value of individual particle and decide if the global peak is found. Meanwhile, the PWM signal is generated to control the MOSFET switching device of the DC–DC converter to extract the PV panel maximum power and deliver it to the load. The electrical reference and solver configuration block are required for the model to run correct. The details of the PV panel, MPPT PSO algorithm and DC–DC converter circuitry are elaborate in the following subsections.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_003.jpg)

Figure 6.3: MPPT PSO system model.

### 6.3.1 PV panel model

PV panels are made of solar cells assembled in series-parallel configuration to generate sufficiently large current and voltage [[33](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_033), [34](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_034), [35](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_035), [36](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_036), [37](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_037), [38](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_038), [39](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_039)]. The basic solar cell is indeed an uncomplicated current source connected in parallel with p-n junction diode [[26](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_026)].

The output of PV cell current, *I*[o] is illustrated by equation (6.3) and photo current, *I*[ph] is associated with sun irradiance by equation (6.4):

(6.3)$I_{o} = N_{p}I_{ph} + N_{p}I_{rs}\left\lbrack {e^{\frac{q{({V + R_{s}I_{o}})}}{AKTN_{s}}} - 1} \right\rbrack - N_{p}\frac{q\left( {V + R_{s}I_{o}} \right)}{N_{s}R_{sh}}$(6.4)$\mspace{-16740mu} I_{ph} = \frac{G}{1,000}\left( {I_{sc} + k_{i}\left( {T - T_{r}} \right)} \right)$

where *N*[p], *N*[s] are number of PV cells connected in parallel and series, *R*[s], *R*[sh] is the series and shunt resistance, *V* is output voltage, *q* is the charge of electron (1.6 × 10^(−19) C), *A* is the ideality factor of the p–n junction*, K* is the Boltzmann’s constant (1.3806503 × 10^(−23) J/K), *k*[i] is the short circuit current coefficient, *T*, *T*[r] is the operating and reference temperature, and lastly *G* is the insolation.

[Figure 6.4](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_004) demonstrates the subsystem of the PV panel model constructed using solar cell and diode blocks from Simscape Electrical Library. It consists of three diodes and three solar cell groups. Each group represents one third area of the panel and it is configured as 20 solar cells connected in series per group. Each group is connected to a bypass diode connected across its positive and negative terminals. The partial shading conditions can be simulated by setting different irradiance inputs (in W/m²) for each group. PV characteristic curve with multiple peaks are generated due to the activation of bypass diode for the shaded solar cell group with lower irradiance.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_004.jpg)

Figure 6.4: PV panel with three solar cell groups and three bypass diodes.

The PV panel models used for this chapter study are based on commercial 250 W 60 cells panel. The short circuit current and open circuit voltage from the datasheet is 8.75 A and 36.6 V, respectively. Since all 60 cells are serially connected, the short circuit current for individual cell is fixed at 8.75 A. On the other hand the open circuit voltage for each cell is set to 0.61 V. The quality factor for each cell is set to 1.216 for monocrystalline cell. The series resistance for each cell is set to 0.0018 ohm. Lastly, the bypass diode is set to have 0.6 V forward voltage drop.

### 6.3.2 DC–DC buck converter

DC–DC buck converter is utilized to step down the input voltage to a required voltage to the load. Koutroulis [40] have developed a MPPT system using DC–DC buck converter together with microcontroller, where voltage is used as reference to locate the GMPP by modifying the duty cycle accordingly. Therefore, is it widely used for standalone solar charge controller application to step down the PV panel voltage to charge the battery load. As shown in [Figure 6.5](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_005) the DC–DC buck converter circuitry is constructed using an inductor, a Schottky diode, two capacitors, and a MOSFET as the main control switching device driven by a gate driver. The operating principle of the buck converter is explained as follows. When the MOSFET is turned on, it draws the power from the PV panel charge the inductor *L* and output capacitor *C*[out] to the load. When MOSFET is turned off, Schottky diode *D* is activated to allow the inductor discharging its current to the load. By switching the MOSFET with a PWM signal, it can control the desired voltage at the load as well as the input resistance seen by the PV panel, hence can determine the power carried to the load. The input resistance of the buck converter *R*[in] seen by the PV panel is governed by equation (6.7), where *R*[out] is the load resistance and *d* is the PWM duty cycle.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_005.jpg)

Figure 6.5: DC–DC buck converter operation zone.

As shown in [Figure 6.5](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_005), *R*[eq(out)] is the equivalent output resistance as seen by the PV module, the operation zone of PV is at right side of IV curve when using buck converter and coverage area is determined by value load resistance used [[29](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_019)]. The use of smaller load resistance allows larger coverage area of the PV curve. The value of *R*[out] can be obtained with equations (6.5) and (6.6) where *V*[MPP(max)] and *I*[MPP(max)] can be obtained from PV panel datasheet, measured at STC-1000 W/m² and 25 °C [[27](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_ref_020)]:

(6.5)$R_{{MPP}{({min})}} = \frac{V_{{MPP}{({max})}}}{I_{{MPP}{({max})}}}$(6.6)*R*[*o**u**t*] < *R*[*M**P**P*(*m**i**n*)](6.7)${}R_{in} = \frac{R_{out}}{d}$

The critical inductance *L* and capacitance *C*[out] values of the buck converter can be approximated by using equations (6.8) and (6.9), respectively, where *f* is the switching frequency:

(6.8)$L > \frac{\left( {1 - d} \right)R_{out}}{2f}$(6.9)$\mspace{-5040mu} C_{out} > \frac{\left( {1 - d} \right)}{16Lf^{2}}$

The switching frequency *f* is set to 7,812.5 Hz. The inductor *L* is set as 1mH to enable the buck converter to work in continuous current mode for wider range of resistive load. The load resistance *R*[out] of 2 Ω is chosen to ensure the entire range of PV curve range can be covered. The output capacitor *C*[out] cannot be set too large to ensure that the initial transient time of the converter is kept at minimum value, therefore *C*[out] is set as 47 µF. The input capacitor *C*[in] is set as 470 µF to smooth out the PV output voltage.

## 6.4 MPPT PSO algorithm block

MPPT PSO algorithm is the main block superior to track the MPP. The subsystem of the MPPT PSO is shown in [Figure 6.6](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_006). It consists of two mean blocks to read the average PV input voltage and current, the PSO algorithm is implemented using MATLAB function block. The PSO algorithm allows the user to set the number of particles, acceleration coefficients of *c*[1] and *c*[2] as well as the inertia weight of *w*. The PSO algorithm block receives the average values of input voltage and current from PV panel through the mean block to search for its maximum power. The output power *P* represents the fitness value of best particle found in every iteration, while the position of each particle represents the duty cycle *D* that used to control the buck converter. The saturation block is used to limit the duty cycle value in between 0.01 and 1\. Lastly, the PWM signal is generated by the PWM generator to control the buck converter for delivering the power to the load. The frequency of PWM generator block is set to 7812.5 Hz. This frequency is chosen to minimize the capacity of the inductor and this is also the more realistic frequency value that can be achieved by the Arduino UNO ATmega328 microcontroller for hardware implementation.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_006.jpg)

Figure 6.6: Subsystem of MPPT PSO block.

The mechanisms of PSO algorithm is explained in a flow chart as presented in [Figure 6.7](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_007). In MPPT PSO algorithm, the particles are first initialized at random across the search space with duty cycle value in between 0.01 and 1\. The initially, personal best position (*P*[best]) of each particle and the global best position (*G*[best]) are set as 0\. The fitness value representing the PV input power of each particles is then is evaluated. For each particle, if the fitness value of its current position is better (i.e., higher) than those of its personal best position, the latter’s position and fitness will be replaced by the former ones. If any of updated *P*[best] has better fitness than those of *G*[best], then the latter’s position and fitness will be replaced by the former once as well. Referring to the updated *P*[best] and *G*[best], the new velocity of all particles will be determined based on equation (6.1). This searching process repeats and all particles are slowly converging toward the global peak power until convergence criteria is met. The convergence criteria met when the differences between maximum power point corresponds to the duty cycle between all particles are less than 0.1\. When the convergence criteria are met, the duty cycle value stored in the *G*[best] position duty will be used. When there are changes of irradiance that leads to the power differences*,* *Δ**P* of more than 5% compared to power from previous iteration, the searching process of MPPT PSO is reinitialized again in order to locate the new global peak:

(6.10)$\Delta P = \frac{\left( {P_{k} - P_{k - 1}} \right)}{P_{k - 1}}\; x\; 100\%$![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_007.jpg)

Figure 6.7: Flowchart of MPPT PSO algorithm.

## 6.5 Results and discussion

For real-world environment, the global peak caused by partial shading can either locate on the left or right side of the power curve and sometimes the distance between the local and global peaks can be extremely close. Therefore, it is crucial to have a robust tracking algorithm that is capable of tracking the GMPP correctly. For PSO-based MPPT algorithm, its tracking effectiveness in view of tracking time, accuracy and robustness are govern by its parameters setting. Extensive performance analyses are hence conducted in this section to identify the most optimized parameters settings the number of particles, acceleration coefficients and inertia weightage that can lead to the optimal tracking performance. The PV power characteristic curves generated under the PSC as shown in [Figure 6.2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002) are utilized to analyze the tracking performance of MPPT PSO. [Figure 6.8(a–f](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_008)) illustrate how the PSO with four particles is able to gradually locate the global peak within 6 iterations. These four particles are initialized randomly between 0 and 1 of duty cycle value as shown in [Figure 6.8(a](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_008)). At each iteration, these particles are gradually moving toward the location of power curve with the highest power as illustrated in [Figure 6.8(b–e](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_008)). When the personal best positions of all particles are within the duty cycle of less than 0.1 as shown in [Figure 6.8(f](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_008)), the convergence criterion is considered met. Global best particle refers to the solution with the best duty cycle value that is capable of locating the GMPP of power curve.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_008.png)

Figure 6.8: MPPT PSO algorithm search for global peaks in six iterations.

### 6.5.1 Performance evaluation of the number of particles

This performance evaluation investigates the influence of the number of particles on tracking time and tracking accuracy of MPPT PSO algorithm. The PV power curve generated under PSC as shown in [Figure 6.2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002) is used for this evaluation, where the maximum power at global peak is recorded as 131.8 W. The parameters of acceleration coefficients are set at *c*[1] = 1, *c*[2] = 1 and the inertia weightage are set as *w *= 0.5\. Meanwhile, the population size considered in this performance evaluation are set between three and eight particles. This is because the PSO with population size lesser than 3 has high tendency to suffer with premature convergence, whereas the population size of 8 tends to have slow convergence speed due to long oscillation process in tracking. The tracking performance of MPPT PSO with 4 particles in one of the attempts are shown in [Figure 6.9](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_009) and it is observed to be capable of tracking the GMPP at 131.8 W within 0.038 s.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_009.jpg)

Figure 6.9: Tracking performance of MPPT PSO with four particles.

Due to the stochastic nature of PSO algorithm, the tracking trajectory, time and error obtained in each tracking attempt can never be exactly the same. Therefore, the MPPT PSO with different population sizes are tested for 10 consecutive attempts in order to ensure its tracking robustness and consistency. [Figure 6.10](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_010) summarizes tracking time of MPPT PSO with different population size for 10 consecutive attempts.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_010.jpg)

Figure 6.10: Tracking time performance for each number of particles in 10 attempts.

It can be observed the MPPT PSO with larger population size have higher tendency to produce longer tracking time. For instance, the MPPT PSO with population size of 7 has produced 2 longest tracking time out of 10 attempts. This is because more time is required by MPPT PSO with larger population size to meet the convergence criterion. Furthermore, the MPPT PSO with larger population size also incur more computation time.

[Figure 6.11](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_011) shows the tracking error performance produced by MPPT PSO with different population sizes within 10 consecutive attempts. It can be observed that the MPPT PSO with population sizes of 3 and 4 tend to produce larger error in 2 attempts. This is because MPPT PSO with smaller population sizes have lesser coverage in the search space and this drawback can lead to the high tendency of premature convergence before the algorithm locate the GMPP. For MPPT PSO with five or more particles, the global peak can be easily located during the first iteration and all particles begin to converge toward the global peak in order to lower error value. Nevertheless, the MPPT PSO with larger population size tends to have slower tracking time due to the poor convergence characteristics.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_011.jpg)

Figure 6.11: Tracking error performance for each number of particles in 10 attempts.

In order to highlight the trade-off between tracking time and error performance, the average values of tracking time and error produced by MPPT PSO with different population sizes are illustrated in [Figure 6.12](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_012). The correlation between tracking time and error shows a diverging trend. However, both tracking time and error are within an acceptable range for real time application. Referring to [Figure 6.12](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_012), it is concluded that the PSO with population sizes between 3 and 5 are suitable to be used for MPPT application.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_012.jpg)

Figure 6.12: Tracking time and error correlation.

### 6.5.2 Performance evaluation on acceleration coefficients

This performance evaluation aims to investigate the influence of acceleration coefficients on both tracking time and accuracy. This is because the optimal parameters settings of acceleration coefficients *c*[1] and *c*[2] may be different according to particular optimization problems. From the velocity update scheme of PSO algorithm as shown in equation (6.1), *c*[1] and *c*[2] are associated with self-cognitive (i.e., personal best position) and social (i.e., global best position) component of PSO swarm, respectively.

Similarly, the PV power curve generated under partial shading conditions as shown in [Figure 6.2 a](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002)re used for performance evaluation. Referring to the simulation results obtained from previous subsection, the population size of MPPT PSO is set as 4, while the value of inertia weightage remains at *w *= 0.5\. Meanwhile, the values of acceleration coefficients are limited between the range of 0.5 and 1.5 because the *c*[1] and *c*[2] with too large values or too notable differences tend to have slow tracking time or even unable to track MPP due to the high oscillation characteristics as shown in [Figure 6.13](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_013). In this performance evaluation, the MPP tracking efficiency of PSO with four different combinations of acceleration coefficients (a) *c*[1]= 0.5, *c*[2] = 1.5, (b) *c*[1]= 0.8, *c*[2]= 1.2, (c) *c*[1]= 1.2, *c*[2]= 0.8, and (d) *c*[1]= 1.5, *c*[2]= 0.5 are analyzed.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_013.jpg)

Figure 6.13: Acceleration coefficients of *c*[1]= 0.2 and *c*[2]= 1.8 that lead to oscillation.

Similarly, the MPPT PSO with different combinations of acceleration coefficients are tested for 10 consecutive attempts and the tracking time achieved for different parameter settings are summarized in [Figure 6.14](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_014). It can be observed that the tracking time performances of MPPT PSO are influenced by the differences between both acceleration coefficients. It is also notable that the MPPT PSO with similar gap between *c*[1] and *c*[2] (i.e., *c*[1]= 0.8 and *c*[2] = 1.2) tends to demonstrate more consistent tracking time performance and vice versa (i.e., *c*[1] = 0.5 and *c*[2] = 1.5). The simulation results in [Figure 6.14](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_014) also reveal the feasibility of MPPT PSO in real-time MPPT application because the tracking time achieved by different combination of acceleration coefficients is in between 0.02 and 0.18 s.

[Figure 6.15](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_015) shows the tracking error performance produced by MPPT PSO with different combinations of acceleration coefficients in 10 consecutive attempts. It is observed that when the MPPT PSO with acceleration coefficients of (a) *c*[1] = 0.5, *c*[2] = 1.5 and (d) *c*[1] = 1.5 and *c*[2] = 0.5 tend to have higher tendency to produce larger tracking errors as shown in [Figure 6.14](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_014). When the acceleration coefficients of *c*[1] and *c*[2] are notably different, the MPPT PSO tends to have higher tendency to suffer with premature convergence due to the poor balancing of exploration and exploitation searches, hence leading to higher tracking error. In contrary, MPPT PSO with acceleration coefficients of (b) *c*[1] = 0.8, *c*[2] = 1.2, and (c) *c*[1] = 1.2, *c*[2] = 0.8 are reported to produce lower tracking errors due to their better balancing of exploration and exploitation strengths in searching for optimal duty cycle that can lead to MPPT. Referring to [Figure 6.15](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_015), it is observed that MPPT PSO is able to produce the lowest tracking errors in majority of attempts when the value of *c*[2] is set slightly higher than *c*[1] in order for MPPT PSO to obtain the lowest tracking errors. In other words, the exploitation search that is contributed by global best position should be emphasized slightly more in order for MPPT PSO to converge toward the global peak with minimum errors.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_014.jpg)

Figure 6.14: Acceleration coefficients tracking time performance.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_015.jpg)

Figure 6.15: Acceleration coefficients tracking error performance.

In order to investigate the compromise between tracking time and error performance, average tracking time and error produced by MPPT PSO with different combinations of acceleration coefficient are shown in [Figure 6.16](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_016). It is observed that the MPPT PSO that has better tracking time tends to produce slightly degraded tracking error and vice versa. However, both of the tracking time and error produced by MPPT PSO with all combination of acceleration coefficients are still within an acceptable range for real-time application. By prioritizing the tracking error in MPPT application, the acceleration coefficient of *c*[1]= 0.8 and *c*[2]= 1.2 are identified as the optimal parameter settings.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_016.jpg)

Figure 6.16: Acceleration coefficients tracking time and error correlation.

### 6.5.3 Performance evaluation on inertia weight

Apart from acceleration coefficients, the velocity of PSO particles in each iteration are also directly influenced by the inertia weight.Therefore, it is important to investigate how inertia weight can affect the tracking time and error in MPPT application. The value of inertia weight should be set the range of 0.1 to 1, according to most PSO literature. In this subsection, the tracking performances of MPPT PSO with different inertia weight values of *w *= 0.1, *w *= 0.3, *w *= 0.5, *w *= 0.8, and *w *= 1 are analyzed. Referring to the simulation results obtained from previous subsections, the population size and acceleration coefficients of MPPT PSO are set as 4 particles as well as *c*[1]= 0.8 and *c*[2]= 1.2 for the following evaluation. Similarly, the PV power curve generated under PSC as shown in [Figure 6.2](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_002) is used for performance evaluation. [Figure 6.17](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_017) shows the tracking time performance obtained by MPPT PSO with different each inertia weight in 10 consecutive attempts.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_017.jpg)

Figure 6.17: Inertia weight tracking time performance.

It is observed that the tracking time of MPPT PSO is directly proportional to the inertia weight value because the larger inertia weight can lead to longer tracking time and vice versa. When inertia weight of MPPT PSO is set to the largest value of *w* = 1.0, the corresponding tracking time obtained is the largest value of 0.4 s. Nevertheless, the overall tracking time performances demonstrated by MPPT PSO with different values of inertia weight are still acceptable for real-time MPPT application.

[Figure 6.18](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_018) shows the tracking error performances produced by MPPT PSO with different inertia weight in 10 consecutive attempts. Similar trends are observed where the MPPT PSO with larger inertia weightage tends to produce larger tracking errors and vice versa. This is notable that the inertia weight is used to govern the exploration strength of MPPT PSO. When large value of inertia weight is set, it implies that the previous velocity of particle plays greater roles in guiding the searching process with greater exploration strength. While the explorative search characteristic might be beneficial in avoiding the premature convergence, it also tends to suffer with overshooting issues that can lead to higher tracking error.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_018.jpg)

Figure 6.18: Inertia weight tracking error performance.

The trade-off between the average tracking time and error performances produced by MPPT PSO with different inertia weight values are shows in [Figure 6.19](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_019). It is observed that both tracking time and errors change in similar trend when the inertia weight value is varied. It is also notable that both tracking time and error produced by MPPT PSO with different inertia weight values are fall within the acceptable ranges for real-time application. Different value of inertia weight should be set for MPPT PSO depending on the system requirements. For instance, the inertia weight of *w* = 0.5 can be set if the tracking error is the main priority of the system. For the system that prioritizes on fast tracking time, the inertia weight of *w* = 0.1 should be considered.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_019.jpg)

Figure 6.19: Inertia weight tracking time and error correlation.

### 6.5.4 Performance evaluation on transition tracking

The varying of solar irradiance intensity due to moving shadow casting on the PV panel can lead to the changes of magnitude in global peak. Depending on the partial shading conditions, the corresponding global peak can be located at any location (i.e., lower or higher voltage regions) of the PV curve. The proposed PSO algorithm will be reinitialized when the irradiance changes aiming to track the new location of the global peak. In this subsection, the tracking robustness of MPPT PSO and its ability to trace the transition of global peak due to the changes of irradiances is evaluated based on the optimal parameter settings of population size (i.e., 4), acceleration coefficients (i.e., *c*[1] = 0.8, *c*[2] = 1.2) and inertia weight (i.e., *w* = 0.1) determined from previous analyses. Four different PV curves used for transition tracking test are shown in [Figure 6.20](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_020), where GMPP1 to GMPP4 represents the PV curves with maximum power of 61.4, 93.1, 64.8, and 31.4 W, respectively. These four PV curves are used to test the robustness of MPPT PSO against different global peaks position at one second per transition.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_020.jpg)

Figure 6.20: Four PV curves for transition tracking evaluation.

Similarly, the transitions tracking were tested for 10 consecutive attempts. The average tracking time and tracking error of all 4 transitions are recorded. The transition tracking performance for one of the attempts is shown in [Figure 6.21](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_021).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_021.png)

Figure 6.21: Transition tracking performance for one of the attempt.

It apparently reported that the MPPT PSO is capable of tracking the first GMPP of 61.36 W in just 0.029 s. For second transition, the MPPT PSO need to be reinitialized for the second transition and it is observed to track the second global peak at 92.93 W successfully in 0.08 s. Similarly observations can be made on the subsequent transition tracking, where the MPPT PSO is able to track the third global peak of 64.77 W and fourth global peak of 31.3 W successfully with 0.078 and 0.064 s, respectively.

[Figure 6.22](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_022) summarized the transition average tracking time performance achieved by MPPT PSO for 10 consecutive attempts. It can be observed that the fastest average tracking time achieved is 0.06 s and the longest average tracking time is 0.18\. The tracking time produced by MPPT PSO is within acceptable range for real-time MPPT application. Because the longest average tracking time produced is still fast enough to adapt with any irradiance changes in the real-world environment.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_022.jpg)

Figure 6.22: Transition average tracking time evaluation.

[Figure 6.23](#9783110713794_014814.xhtml_b_9783110714043-006_fig_023) summarizes the transition average tracking error performance achieved by MPPT PSO for 10 consecutive attempts. It can be observed that the MPPT PSO is able to achieve the lowest average error values of 0.15%. In contrary, the highest average tracking error produced is only 6.6%. While there are still room for improvement to further reduce the average tracking error, the simulation results disclose that the tracking performance of MPPT PSO are sufficiently good for real-time MPPT application.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-006_fig_023.jpg)

Figure 6.23: Transition average tracking error evaluation.

In summary, the optimal parameter settings determined based on previous performance evaluation are proven effective for PSO to perform MPPT tracking under dynamic changing partial shading conditions without failing for 10 consecutive attempts. Last, the MPPT PSO model in presented in this chapter is brought accessible by the authors meant for reader to download at Mathworks official MATLAB central file exchange link:

[https://www.mathworks.com/matlabcentral/fileexchange/87142-mppt-tracking-based-on-particle-swarm-optimization](https://www.mathworks.com/matlabcentral/fileexchange/87142-mppt-tracking-based-on-particle-swarm-optimization)

## 6.6 Conclusion

An MPPT PSO algorithm with DC–DC buck converter developed with MATLAB/Simulink is presented in current study. PV panel with multiples peaks power characteristic curve generated by partial shading are used to evaluate the tracking performance. The tracking performances of MPPT PSO are evaluated based on different combinations of parameters inclusive of the population size, acceleration coefficients and inertia weight. The simulation results obtained by MPPT PSO with different parameter settings are analyzed. It is reported that the populations size of 4, inertia weight of 0.1, as well as acceleration coefficients of *c*[1] = 0.8 and *c*[2] = 1.2 are the optimal parameter settings of PSO used in current study. Lastly, the tracking robustness of MPPT PSO with optimal parameters setting is also evaluated with dynamic changing partial shading conditions. The best average tracking time and tracking error recorded by the proposed method are 0.06 s and 0.15%, respectively, implying its feasibility for real-time application. This chapter provides a comprehensive insight on how PSO parameters can affect the MPPT performance in terms of tracking time, accuracy, and stability required for practical tracking application.
