# 7 Wireless monitoring of substation using IoT

Varsha SinghNational Institute of Technology, Raipur, IndiaSimran BajajNational Institute of Technology, Raipur, IndiaShreyash GanvirNational Institute of Technology, Raipur, IndiaSwapnil SahuNational Institute of Technology, Raipur, IndiaMahendra GavelNational Institute of Technology, Raipur, India

#### Abstract

Internet of things (IoT) is becoming a revolutionary technology in transforming the modern standard of living. IoT allows integration of data acquisition, communication and processing in real time through remote servers. This chapter discusses a wireless system for monitoring and controlling of an electrical substation using NodeMCU (Wi-Fi module), Arduino Uno (microcontroller), Thing Speak Server, and Blynk. The different parameters of the substation such as current, voltage, power factor, frequency, and temperature are monitored using various sensors and electronic components. The concept is validated using an experimental setup. NodeMCU is the control center of the system that sends the data to Thing Speak server and the remote user could take action accordingly. Depending upon the data received, the end user could control circuit breakers and relays allowing optimum control over the system in real time. The accurate data gathered is useful in analysis, optimum planning and detecting faults. The real-time feature of IoT allows taking control action quickly, thereby reducing time delay and risks. The innovation of the work can bring efficient monitoring and controlling of an electrical substation. Mobile apps are very easy to use, and this work helps controlling and visualization of5 electrical substation in future.

Keywords: NodeMCU, hingSpeak, PulseIn, Arduino, substation, IoT, Blynk,

## 7.1 Introduction

Internet of things or IoT allows various machines, devices or appliances to connect with each other to communicate, access, and modify data using the Internet without the involvement of humans. Industrial IoT is considered to be vital in smart technology as per Industry 4.0. The applications of IoT are widespread such as in transport, agriculture, health care, and industries [[1](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_001), [2](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_002), [3](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_003), [4](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_004)]. IoT allows the user to track, monitor and take better decisions. Automation decreases the workload and increases efficiency.

IoT has made tremendous progress in various domains, specifically in electrical engineering. Smart grids and smart meters use this technology to provide real-time data to the user for monitoring purposes [[5](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_005), [6](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_006)]. Various electrical devices such as speakers, smart lights come with IoT-enabled features providing better end-user experience. In the renewable energy sector, IoT is proving to be extremely beneficial. Since the solar and wind farms are situated in remote places, data monitoring and analysis is done through IoT [[7](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_007)]. Using the data provided, users may optimize the maintenance schedule, balance the supply demand curve, manage loads, and predict future energy generation. Renewable energy sector is getting more efficient and reliable using IoT technology [[8](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_008)].

The traditional grids in electrical systems are becoming IoT enabled making them smart grids. Smart grids allow data-driven decisions to optimize electricity generation. The small electrical circuits are now made with IoT-enabled features for easy monitoring and control. The hardware as well as the software for these circuits is cheap and readily available in the market. Data from these various devices is processed and sent to the cloud server via the Internet. The end user receives this data for analysis and monitoring. The user might even take control action.

Industrial IoT (IIoT) has been extensively used in various systems of electrical engineering. An IoT-based smart sustainable solution applied in electric power and energy systems (EPESs) has been discussed in [[9](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_009)]. The various challenges of IoT in power and energy systems have been discussed in [[10](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_010)]. The major advantage that IoT imparts is the real-time feedback capabilities to the utilities which function to better serve customers through enhanced monitoring and control functionalities [[10](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_010)]. Sharif et al. [[11](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_011)] propose and optimize a fault-tolerant scheduling algorithm for fog computing modules. In [[12](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_012)], a low-cost smart electric meter is studied for the measurement of electrical parameters. The meter is capable of adapting to the variability of the grid maintaining a high level of measurement accuracy.

In some literature the use of IoT for monitoring power substations is discussed. In [[13](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_013)], the design of IIoT-based monitoring system using FPGA (Field Programmable Gate Arrays) is discussed with recording features. Automation of an 11 kV substation using raspberry pi and Codesys 61131-3 software is discussed in [[14](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_014)]. IoT systems can also be used in predicting maintenance in substations. In [[15](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_015)], a smart connector is used to power a microcontroller, that controls transmitter and sensors to estimate electrical contact resistance (ECR) of the connector [[16](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_016), [17](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_017), [18](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_018), [19](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_019), [20](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_020), [21](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_021), [22](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_022), [23](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_023), [24](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_024)].

The application of IoT covers almost a breadth of every technical advancement. In literature [[25](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_025), [26](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_026), [27](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_027)], the applications of IoT in medical fields have been discussed. In [[25](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_025)], a smart medical service system is proposed to increase medical resource utilization and improve the efficiency of medical processes. In [[26](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_026)], ideas to integrate and apply IoT ideas in the healthcare sector have been discussed. In [[27](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_027)], an IoT-based Smart Health care box for elderly people has been designed, so that, right medicine can be taken at the right time. Literature [[28](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_028), [29](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_029), [30](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_030), [31](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_031), [32](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_032)] discusses the applications in the mechanical field. In [[28](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_028)], mechanical fault prediction systems are discussed, by continuously monitoring electric motors. In [[29](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_029)], the paper describes an IoT-based engine oil sludge monitoring device and its operational methodology.

IoT is expanding exponentially in the electrical domain. One such application of IoT is in monitoring remote substations. ID Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) has historically been used to monitor and control remote substations with dedicated communication lines that usually run on the side of power lines and dedicated wired remote control circuits for substations. These days it is hard to find errors in the transmission of power through substation due to errors like overheating of transformers, over current through lines over the increase in power factor of supply [[33](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_033), [34](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_034), [35](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_035), [36](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_036), [37](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_037), [38](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_038), [39](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_039), [40](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_040), [41](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_041), [42](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_042)]. These errors result in shut down for many hours because it is hard to find the problem element in the substation. These problems can be sorted out easily by knowing the value of each parameter with the help of sensors. These parameters can be monitored wirelessly with the help of NodeMCU (Wi-Fi module) [[43](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_043), [44](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_044), [45](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_045)].

Real-time data of these values is transferred wirelessly to a server. Then it is easy to monitor the error area by comparing which increased value caused the error. For example, if the voltage across the transformer is measured greater than the rated value of the transformer then there will be an automatic system which will operate the circuit breaker and prevent transformer damage and unwanted power cut. The implementation of this is achieved with the help of sensors like voltage sensor (voltage divider), current sensor (ACS712), Schmitt trigger, and XoR gate for measurement of frequency and power factor. These values are measured by NodeMCU and are continuously transferred to a server where a person can analyze these data and take the required action. These values of voltage and current can be further analyzed to predict the power needed according to months and improve power generation.

The chapter is organized in the following sections:

Section 7.2 discusses the implementation of the methodology with a block diagram and flowchart. Section 7.3 describes the components used. Implementation through hardware is shown in Section 7.4 followed by results in Section 7.5\. Section 7.6 is a summary of the chapter followed by references.

## 7.2 Implementation of proposed methodology

The implementation of IoT in substation is demonstrated in various stages in the form of block diagram as shown in [Figure 7.1](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_001). The block diagram consists of a microcontroller (Arduino Uno), a Wi-Fi module (NodeMCU), LCD screen, various sensors, transformer, and relays.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_001.jpg)

Figure 7.1: Block diagram of the proposed system.

Before the understanding of the process of wireless monitoring of the substation, the application and usage of each of the blocks is crucial. The purpose of the transformer is to step down the line voltage from 220 to 12 V. This is done so that the values may be under the permissible limit for measuring. The different parameters such as current, voltage, frequency, power factor, and temperature are measured through sensors using proper circuitry. Sensors and the circuit used for measuring the various parameters are discussed in detail in the [next section](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_s_007_s_002).

The measured data is fed to the microcontroller, that is, Arduino Uno. A microcontroller is a compact integrated circuit that is designed to perform specific operations. A microcontroller consists of a microprocessor, memory and input–output peripherals. The Arduino Uno is an open-source microcontroller board based on a microchip ATmega328P microcontroller and developed by Arduino.cc. The IC is equipped with various analog and digital pins and can be coded with Arduino IDE.

It accepts data from all the sensors and displays the information in the LCD screen for local monitoring.

Further, the microcontroller is connected with the Wi-Fi module, that is, NodeMCU. NodeMCU is an open-source firmware and development board designed for IoT-based applications. The NodeMCU is responsible for sending the data to the cloud storage. It uses TCP/IP protocol to do this. The user is now able to access the entire information remotely from anywhere in the world via the Internet. NodeMCU can be configured with an open-source platform for data visualization called ThingSpeak.

ThingSpeak is an IoT analytics platform service that allows us to aggregate, visualize and analyze information in the cloud [[33](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_033)]. The real-time data of the substation such as the temperature, voltage, current, power factor, and frequency is visualized in the Thing Speak cloud platform. As required, data can be configured as public or private.

Relays and circuit breakers are connected to the NodeMCU for the controlling of the substation. Relays are electronic switches through which users may ensure proper control over the substation. The user can remotely instruct for the relay to act and perform a certain action. For example: If the temperature of the transformer exceeds a particular threshold, the user may instruct to switch the transformer fan using a relay remotely. This ensures a good control over the substation.

### 7.2.1 System flowchart

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_002.jpg)

Figure 7.2: System flowchart.

In [Figure 7.2](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_002), the flowchart shows the entire technique adopted and the sequence.

First, the voltage is stepped down to 12 V using a transformer. A full wave rectifier circuit is connected in parallel to the transformer‘s output. This converts the AC voltage to DC voltage. It is further passed to a voltage divider circuit and measured directly from the analog pin in Arduino. The entire explanation of voltage measurement is discussed in [Section 7.3.3.](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_s_007_s_002)

A current sensor ACS712 is connected in series from the output of the transformer. The output of it is passed to the Schmitt trigger for the measurement of frequency(discussed in Section 7.3.4) and XOR gate for the measurement of power factor(discussed in [Section 7.3.5](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_s_007_s_002)).

Temperature sensor DHT-11 is used for measuring the temperature of the transformer. The data is received in Arduino and NodeMCU. Using the open-source platform ThingSpeak and Blynk the data stored in the cloud is accessed and visualized in the form of graphs. Relay and circuit breaker are also connected for controlling the substation. The data is locally displayed in LCD Screen as well.

## 7.3 Components used

### 7.3.1 Transformer

A transformer is a type of static electrical device that converts electrical power to magnetic energy and again to electrical energy. In electric systems and transmission lines, a step-down transformer has a wide range of applications [[40](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_040), [41](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_041), [42](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_042)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_003.jpg)

Figure 7.3: Transformer.

A transformer as shown in [Figure 7.3](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_003) is used to step-down the 230 V voltage to 12 V for the project with all the sensors used. The sensors and controllers used in the circuit require 5 V supply. The IC used in the circuit also works on low voltage which is further step downed using resistor pairs. Transformer used in this project is 12-0-12 V which can be used as a 12 or 24 V voltage output. A 12-0-12 V is of center-tapped transformer type. This transformer is normally used for rectifier circuits. This transformer is connected to a rectifier circuit and then AC mains signal is converted to DC source which is then used in sensor and IC input. The transformer is also used to limit mains supply which protects us from higher shocks. The current rating for the transformer is 5 A which is then used in the resistor and inductor circuit which helps in providing appropriate current for measuring the power factor. Another transformer is used in the project as an extra inductor. The transformer can be coveted to work as an inductor by interchanging the output wires and can provide the good inductance in the circuit. In this project we change the resistance using the rheostat and the variable power factor can be seen in DSO (Digital Signal Oscilloscope) output.

### 7.3.2 Current measurement

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_004.jpg)

Figure 7.4: Current sensor (ACS712).

Measurement of current is an important step in monitoring of a substation. If access current flows through the load, an overload condition might arise, thereby damaging the devices. Current can be measured in two ways: Direct measurement and indirect measurement. In the direct method, Ohm’s law is used. The voltage drop over the wire is measured when the current flows through it. For given wire resistance, the current is calculated. In this method, the current is measured by applying either Faraday’s law or Ampere law. The current-carrying conductor gives rise to a magnetic field in its surroundings [[4](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_004)]. A Hall effect sensor or a transformer are used to measure this magnetic field.

With the ACS712 current sensor, the current flowing through the load is measured. It is a linear sensor based on Hall effect. Along with a low-resistance current conductor, it has a 2.1 kV RMS voltage insulation. Since this sensor is based on the Hall effect, it is an example of the process of indirect current sensing. The IC contains a low-offset, Hall effect sensor through which the current flows and generates a magnetic field. This sensor is located on some kind of copper path at the IC’s surface. It produces a magnetic field that is detected by the Hall effect sensor when the current flows through such a conduction channel. The Hall sensor, used to calculate current, produces a voltage equal to the detected magnetic field [[16](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_016)].

The proximity of the magnetic signal to the Hall sensor determines the accuracy of the device. The closer the magnetic signal is, the higher the accuracy. The sensor requires a 5 V power supply. The rating of the sensor is 5A, having sensitivity of 185 mV/A. It is connected in series with the circuit. [Figure 7.4](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_004) shows the current sensor ACS712\. The sensor is powered via a microcontroller.

### 7.3.3 Voltage measurement

Measurement of voltage can be directly done through the microcontroller itself. Unlike the current measurement it does not require any additional sensor for it.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_005.jpg)

Figure 7.5: Voltage divider circuit.

A voltage divider circuit is shown in [Figure 7.5](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_005). A microcontroller first converts the analog voltage to digital using an Analog to Digital Converter. Arduino-Uno, the microcontroller used for this experimental setup has 6-channel, 10-bit ADC numbered from A0 to A5\. The input voltage from 0 to 5 V is mapped from 0 to 1,023, implying the resolution to be 4.9 mV per unit.

For voltages above 5 V, an additional voltage divider circuit is used. It consists of 2 resistors connected in series, that is, R1 and R2\. The voltage to be measured (Vm) is linked to one end of this set of resistors connections and the other end to zero potential. The voltage at the junction of the two resistors (V1) is measured using a microcontroller. It will be proportional to the required voltage (Vm). It is given by

(7.1)*V*[1] = *V*[*m*] × (*R*[2] ÷ (*R*[1] + *R*[2]))… ![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_006.jpg)

Figure 7.6: Rectifier circuit.

The input in this case is 12 V AC. For its measurement, the AC should be converted to DC voltage. The AC to DC conversion is achieved by a full wave rectifier circuit as shown in [Figure 7.6](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_006). The full wave rectifier circuit consists of four diodes and a capacitor for filtering. The input to this circuit is 12 V AC, and the output is 12 V DC. The output from the rectifier circuit is connected to the voltage divider circuit consisting of two resistors. Further, it is tapped at the junction and connected to the microcontroller.

### 7.3.4 Frequency measurement

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_007.jpg)

Figure 7.7: Schmitt trigger (IC 7414).

Frequency is an important parameter that has to be continuously monitored because every AC appliance in India works in 50 Hz and deviation from this frequency severely affects the performance and working of the appliance. The frequency is measured using the microcontroller (Arduino Uno) and Schmitt Trigger (IC SN74LS14) can be seen in [Figure 7.7](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_007).

Schmitt Trigger is a comparator circuit that makes use of positive feedback to implement hysteresis [[20](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_020)]. It is used to remove noise from an analog signal while converting it to a digital.

In this chapter, it used to convert the input signal to square wave. Schmitt trigger is extremely safe to use with Arduino Uno since it is a 5 V IC and the output current is below 40 mA. The time period of the output square wave is the same as the input wave; hence, the frequency does not change. The output waveform of Schmitt trigger is given in [Figure 7.8](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_008).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_008.png)

Figure 7.8: Output of Schmitt trigger.

The frequency is calculated by the formula, *f* = 1/*T*, where *T* is the time period of one cycle. The time period is calculated using the PulseIn function in Arduino. PulseIn function takes in two parameters, that is, the pin number and value. The value could be either high or low. The function returns the length of the pulse in microseconds. For instance, if value is high, the function waits for the signal to go high, starts timing, then waits for the signal to go low and then stops timing.

The total time period of one cycle is the sum of high time and low time:

(7.2)*T*[*o**n*]= *p**u**l**s**e* *h**i**g**h* = *P**u**l**s**e**I**n*(*P**i**n*, *H**I**G**H*)…(7.3)*T*[*o**f**f*] = *p**u**l**s**e* *l**o**w*  = *P**u**l**s**e**I**n*(*P**i**n*, *L**O**W*)…(7.4) *T* = *T*[*o**n*] + *T*[*o**f**f*]  + …

The frequency of the square wave is calculated by the formula:

(7.5)*f**r**e**q**u**e**n**c**y* = 10⁶ ÷ (*p**u**l**s**e* *h**i**g**h* + *p**u**l**s**e* *l**o**w*)…

### 7.3.5 Power-factor measurement

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_009.jpg)

Figure 7.9: XOR (IC 7486).

Power factor is the ratio between true power to the apparent power. It can be calculated by finding the cosine of the phase angle between the supply voltage and current [[17](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_ref_017)]. Measurement of power factor is done by using Schmitt trigger and XOR gate IC 7486\. The voltage and current wave-forms are converted into square waves using Schmitt trigger (ST). The two square waves are passed through the XOR gate using IC 7486 ([Figure 7.9](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_009) is X-OR IC 7486). The output from the XOR gate is 1 or high when the signals have different values. If one is high, then the other should be low and vice versa. The output is 0 or low when the signals have the same value, either both of them are low or both of them are high.

The output is high either when the current waveform is high and voltage waveform is low or vice versa as shown in [Figure 7.8](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_008). Hence, the output is the time difference between the two waves from the point they cross the zero point. The time duration is measured using the Pulse In function as discussed in Section 7.2.4\. The power factor calculation is done as

(7.6)*c**o**s**ϕ* = *d**t* × *f**r**e**q**u**e**n**c**y* × 360…(7.7)*c**o**s**ϕ* = (*p**u**l**s**e* *w**i**d**t**h* × 10^(−6)) × 360 × *f**r**e**q**u**e**n**c**y*…

### 7.3.6 Temperature measurement

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_010.jpg)

Figure 7.10: Temperature sensor (DHT 11).

The temperature of the transformer is measured using a DHT11 sensor shown in [Figure 7.10](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_010). The sensor DHT-11 measures the temperature as well as the humidity. It has three pins, namely, Vcc, data and ground. The operating voltage of this sensor is 3.5–5.5 V. The operating current is 0.3 mA (measuring) and 60 µA (standby). It uses a thermistor and a capacitive humidity sensor to measure the surrounding air. The temperature range is 0 degree to 50 degree and the humidity range is 20–90%. The accuracy of the sensor is ±1 degree. The output of the sensor is given to the digital pin of Arduino. Using the DHT library the output data of humidity and temperature is bifurcated.

### 7.3.7 Relay

A relay is used for purposes which triggers a particular signal which changes the output of the circuit in connection. Relay mainly operates in two modes as normally open and normally closed mode. The normally open is a bridge between the connection which means the path in the connection is open and closed means the connection between two points in the relay in closed and electric current flows between these points.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_011.jpg)

Figure 7.11: Relay.

The construction of the relay module is very simple ([Figure 7.11](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_011)). This consists of a coil, with one input as the 5 V DC supply and the other end of the coil and connected to the switching device in our case the switching device is Arduino Uno. A fly back diode is connected across the coil, to protect the switching device from the spike voltage. The other side of the coil has the two main operating points: normally open (NO) and normally closed (NC), which are then moved by the switching circuit and it triggers the coil and the magnetic force from the coil forces the needle to switch from normally open to normally closed or vice versa.

In the proposed work an eight-channel 5 V relay (FBA_PCB board) has been used. The relay is given a 5 V supply from the rectifier and relay. Normally open (NO) and normally closed (NC) points are connected to the transmission line. Relay is triggered when there is fault in the transmission line or transformer is overheated. The relay’s signaling points are connected to NodeMCU, which helps us control the relays wirelessly through the internet.

### 7.3.8 Microcontroller

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_012.jpg)

Figure 7.12: Arduino UNO.

A microcontroller is a single-chip IC used in an embedded system to perform a particular operation. A simple microcontroller contains a processor, memory unit and input output pins. For the wireless monitoring of substation, Arduino Uno is used.

Arduino Uno board is based on 8-bit ATmega328P microcontroller (shown in [Figure 7.12](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_012)). The brain of the board is the 8-bit processor. Along with it, other components such as a reset button, voltage regulator and oscillator are present to provide additional functionalities. For user interface it contains input output pins. In total, 14 digital input/output pins, 6 analog pins. Out of the 14 digital pins, 6 pins can be used for. To program the board, Arduino IDE is required, which can be easily downloaded from its official website. The IC can also establish communication using the Rx, Tx pin. It can communicate with a computer and other microcontrollers.

### 7.3.9 NodeMCU

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_013.jpg)

Figure 7.13: NodeMCU.

NodeMCU is an open-source firmware and development board ([Figure 7.13](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_013)). It includes firmware that runs on the ESP8266 Wi-Fi, and hardware which is based on the ESP-12 module. It is widely used for IoT applications. The operating voltage of NodeMCU is 3.3 V. The clock speed is 80 MHz. It has 128 KB RAM and 4 MB. It has 11 digital input output pins and 1 analog pin. It has high processing power with in-built Wi-Fi. It is also compatible with Arduino IDE. Micro-USB can be connected to power up NodeMCU.

### 7.3.10 ThingSpeak

ThingSpeak is an open-source application that allows users to store and retrieve data over the internet. It is widely used for IoT applications. It allows us to visualize and analyze data in the cloud. IoT devices such as NodeMCU can communicate with ThingSpeak in order to visualize and analyze the live data.

Uploading data from NodeMCU to ThingSpeak server is achieved using ThingSpeak Write keys. There are many channels on the ThingSpeak server and the channel is identified with the particular Write Key and Channel ID for uploading data. These channels can be accessed from anywhere with particular tags that the user provides so that other users can view the channel. Mentioning Write Key and Channel ID in the code of Arduino IDE, the Wi-Fi module sends the particular data to a server on a specified id.

### 7.3.11 Blynk

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_014.jpg)

Figure 7.14: Blynk.

Blynk ([Figure 7.14](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_014)) is a platform which allows iOS and Android devices to create interfaces for controlling and monitoring hardware projects. Mobile devices can view the sensor data. The data can be managed and visualized easily by users. Users will create a dashboard for the project and organize on-screen buttons, sliders, graphs, and other widgets.

## 7.4 Hardware setup

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_015.jpg)

Figure 7.15: Experimental Setup.

The hardware setup is shown in [Figure 7.15](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_015). All the equipment used and theirs rating are shown in [Table 7.1](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_tab_001).

The transformer first steps down the 220 V to 12 V and the output is connected to the load. The load consists of a variable resistance (Rheostat) and an Inductive load. A current sensor (ACS712) is connected in series with the load, which gives the reading to Arduino. The output from the current sensor is given to the Schmitt trigger to measure frequency, and later to IC 7486 (XOR gate) to measure power factor.

A full-wave rectifier circuit is connected in parallel to the load which is further given to a voltage divider circuit, such that the voltage becomes less than 5 V, and given to an analog Arduino pin. The temperature sensor reads the temperature and humidity value of the transformer and sends it to the microcontroller. Relay and circuit breaker are also connected in the circuit such that a user has complete control over the power system. Arduino processes all the data and sends it to NodeMCU (Wi-Fi module). The NodeMCU sends the data to cloud. ThingSpeak server further displays the data by fetching from the cloud using the ThingSpeak ID. Circuit diagram of experiment setup is illustrated in [Figure 7.16](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_016).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_016.jpg)

Figure 7.16: Circuit diagram.

The Equipment used and description is given in [Table 7.1](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_tab_001).

Table 7.1:Sensors and their specifications.

| Instruments | Specifications | No. of pins | Voltage/current |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Current measurement** | ACS712 | 3 | Voltage – 5 V Current – 5 A
Sensitivity – 185 mA/V |
| **Voltage measurement** | Rectifier circuit | 2 | Voltage – 0–5 V Current – 5 A |
| **Frequency measurement** | Schmitt Trigger (IC 7414) | 14 | Voltage – 5.5 V Current – 8 mA |
| **Power factor measurement** | XOR (iC 7486) | 14 | Voltage – 7 V Current – 8 mA |
| **Temperature measurement** | DHT-11 | 3 | Voltage – 3.3 V Current – 0.3 mA |

## 7.5 Results

The output waveform for all the parameters is measured using a DSO (digital signal oscilloscope).

### 7.5.1 Current measurement

The current waveform shown in [Figure 7.17](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_017) is achieved across the current sensor ACS712\. Current is measured with an ACS712 current sensor. The waveform shows peak-to-peak and rms value of current, with the help of this peak-to-peak voltage measured across the sensor current through line is calculated. The output of this sensor is a sine wave across 2.35 V which can be seen in the image. The output swings from peak to peak giving all positive values which are fed to Arduino. Arduino only measures positive values and with the help of these values current is calculated. The sine changes its peak-to-peak values as we change the values of inductance and resistance in the circuit; this can be assumed as the values coming from the power station and accurate current can be measured.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_017.jpg)

Figure 7.17: Current waveform.

### 7.5.2 Voltage measurement

The voltage waveform is shown in [Figure 7.18](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_018). The RMS value of the waveform is 11.5 V and the peak-to-peak value is 35.6 V as shown. Voltage across transformers is measured with the help of a simple rectifier circuit. The output shows very accurate reading of the input voltage. The image shows reading across the secondary terminal of the transformer. This reading is then converted into actual reading through a rectifier circuit and the same can be seen in the online services. The output of rectifier circuit voltage is mapped accordingly with the primary voltage of the transformer or the voltage coming from the power station.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_018.jpg)

Figure 7.18: Voltage waveform.

### 7.5.3 Frequency measurement

The frequency is measured with Schmitt trigger IC(73HC14). The Schmitt trigger IC converts the input sine wave to square wave. This IC consists of op-amp which helps in converting the sine wave to square wave. Then this square wave is faded to Arduino which measures the number of high and low in the wave in one minute which gives us the frequency of the supply. The Schmitt trigger accepts very low voltage at its input pins. Therefore, the voltage coming from the supply is first passed through a resistor, this resistor acts as a pulldown resistor and then the lower voltage is finally read by the Schmitt trigger. The square wave output can be seen in the power factor image also.

The waveform to calculate frequency is shown in [Figure 7.19](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_019). The blue waveform is achieved across the Schmitt trigger IC and the yellow waveform is the input waveform for the IC. The PulseIn function calculates the duration of the rectangular waveform when it is high and low.

*p**u**l**s**e* *h**i**g**h* = *P**u**l**s**e**I**n*(*P**i**n*, *H**I**G**H*)

*p**u**l**s**e* *l**o**w* = *P**u**l**s**e**I**n*(*P**i**n*, *L**O**W*)

*f**r**e**q**u**e**n**c**y* = 10⁶ (*p**u**l**s**e* *h**i**g**h* + *p**u**l**s**e* *l**o**w*)![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_019.jpg)

Figure 7.19: Waveform to calculate frequency.

### 7.5.4 Power factor measurement

The power factor of the circuit can be seen as the purple spikes which show the difference between the current and voltage phases. For this we use the XOR gate to calculate that gap. The input of XoR gate is current and voltage waveforms. These waveforms are first converted to square waves and then as the input to the XOR gate IC. The square waves as 0 and 1 to XOR are given and the output is 1 for XOR on 01 or 10. Hence, where there is difference in current and voltage waveforms the output 1 as purple spike can be seen. This spike horizontally expands if the inductance is varied and thus shows the results accurately. This output is given to Arduino and power factor is calculated through formulae.

The waveform to calculate Power factor is shown in [Figure 7.20](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_020). In the waveform, the blue waveform is the voltage waveform and the yellow waveform is the current waveform. Using IC 7486 for XOR gate, the purple waveform is achieved. By calculating the duration for which the waveform is high using PulseIn function, the power factor is calculated as explained in Section 7.3.4.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_020.jpg)

Figure 7.20: Waveform to calculate power factor.

All of the measured data is shown in a ThingSpeak Server as shown in [Figure 7.21](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_021).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_021.jpg)

Figure 7.21: Data visualization in ThingSpeak.

It can be easily monitored and visualized. The ThingSpeak window consists of four graphical representations of the transformer temperature, the frequency, output voltage and current. All the data received is the real-time data fetched after a very small time delay (few milliseconds). The same data is seen on a Blynk app as shown in [Figure 7.22](#9783110713794_018026.xhtml_b_9783110714043-007_fig_022). Two switches, namely, circuit breaker switch and Relay switch is shown in the app. The user can manually operate the circuit breaker or relay if and when required.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-007_fig_022.jpg)

Figure 7.22: Data shown in Blynk App.

## 7.6 Conclusion

Wireless substation monitoring using the IoT has been shown to operate adequately in an experimental way. Not only does the designed device track sensor data, such as voltage, current, frequency, power factor, and temperature, but it also activates a process as needed. The relays connected to the transformer were remotely controlled through the Internet. Another added advantage of the system is the data captured. Using proper data analysis a lot of information and prediction could be done. Data can later be used to predict power generation at various power plants. The reach of wireless control can be extended for switchgear and sensors can be used to monitor various parameters of the Power Plant. Automatic capacitor banks can be installed in the power generation side so that when reactive power is required then these capacitor banks can be used automatically and power can be neutralized. The system can be extended for various other purposes such as weather stations or power generation stations using this as a basis. This type of system can be the best solution where human intervention is difficult or unsafe so that no human presence is needed for monitoring and different parameters can be automatically monitored.

# 8 Smart grid–based big data analytics using machine learning and artificial intelligence: a survey

Subin KoshyElectrical Engineering Department, National Institute of Technology, Calicut, IndiaS. RahulElectrical Engineering Department, National Institute of Technology, Calicut, IndiaR. SunithaElectrical Engineering Department, National Institute of Technology, Calicut, IndiaElizabeth P. CheriyanElectrical Engineering Department, National Institute of Technology, Calicut, India

#### Abstract

Exhilarating developments in the renewable energy generation portfolio and the widespread introduction of microgrids have contributed to significant reforms in the existing power grid’s power flow patterns. Power grids around the world have upgraded to smart grid (SG) by introducing advanced monitoring technologies such as Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI), smart meters and Phasor Measurement Units (PMUs), which collect high-resolution electrical measurements across the system. This has brought in a massive amount of data, termed big data, that needs to be efficiently processed to derive valuable insights. Specifically, technical sophistication, security, and integration of datasets are the main concerns that need to be tackled to transform the massive dataset into useful insights. This chapter surveys big data analytics (BDA) and its related benefits, challenges, and advancements in SG’s perspective. BDA in combination with visualization tools, help in the predictive decision-making process in the SG. Thus, data analytics play a significant part in the efficient monitoring of the SG. Powered with the integration of information and communication technologies, an information layer has been introduced to the traditional power systems to collect, store, and process data from smart meters and sensor implementations. Big data architecture involves data aggregation, storing, and analytics, which integrates the SG’s need for a range of frameworks with outstanding computational skills to respond to the customer’s needs. Characterization of big data, SGs, and massive volumes of data processing is first addressed as a preface to demonstrate the motivation and possible benefits of integrating advanced data mining in smart grids. Specific principles and standard data analytics techniques for general concerns are also discussed. The chapter’s key section discusses the advanced uses of various data analytics in SG, leveraging machine learning (ML) and artificial intelligence (AI). SG is benefited from the inherent capacity of ML to generalize, delivering reliable and quick power flow predictions from dispersed measurement units, with superior computing performance and interoperability. The chapter explores numerous literary works that utilize various ML methods to enhance SG activity and management. We will present big data analytics and cloud infrastructure in this chapter and address their importance to SG. Specifically, we would concentrate on relevant computational concerns and solutions linked to SG cyber-physical protection and safety.

Keywords: artificial intelligence, big data analytics, machine learning, smart grid,

## 8.1 Introduction

Smart grid (SG) involves incorporating information and digital communication frameworks in the existing power grid to facilitate bidirectional communication and power flow, thereby improving the power system’s security, reliability, and efficiency [[1](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_001)]. SG also includes the introduction of smart (automated, real-time, digital systems that enhance the device’s performance) metering, communications concerning grid operations and status, and distributed automation. Big data and related analytics have been involved since supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems became common. The abundance of sensors scattered around a vast geographic region offers large volume of data for monitoring purposes. Thus, the SG era introduced large quantity of data, not just from the sensors, but also from the improved embedded computing facilities used in conventional power equipment [[2](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_002)]. This was not just restricted to operational data because as device expertise grew, effective monitoring of real-time computing systems became very essential for coordinating a distributed computing environment. Big data is defined as the term for massive, comprehensive, and unfiltered data sets that cannot be appropriately analyzed with conventional data processing applications [[3](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_003)]. There is an agreement between multiple explanations for big data. This is an evolving technological challenge introduced by the massive dataset of different categories and complicated strategies that require new frameworks and techniques to efficiently excavate valuable insights from the data. Data mining techniques rely on hardware and software to effectively handle the big data effectively [[4](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_004)]. The properties of big data are commonly described using the “five V’s of big data,” as shown in [Table 8.1](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_001).

Table 8.1:Main features of big data involving smart grid.

| Property | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Volume | Large amount of data involved |
| Variety | Different data types involved |
| Veracity | Uncertainty in the data stream |
| Value | Extracting valuable insights from the data |
| Velocity | Rate at which the data flows |

Easier access to mathematical analysis tools, data analytics and signal processing makes it easier to derive valuable insights from the big data. Big data analytics (BDA) involves analytic algorithms, and other data processing approaches that process the available data to obtain the output in the required form. Generally, SG-based big data analytics can be subdivided into four types as follows.

1.  Descriptive analytics – Deals with what is happening around with the SG.

2.  Diagnostic analytics – Deals with the reason as to why it is happening and tries to determine the exact cause behind the SG event.

3.  Predictive analytics – Deals with estimating the outcomes likely to occur based on the historical data and with a certain confidence level.

4.  Prescriptive analytics – Deals with what strategies are to be adopted and decisions that are to be driven based on the insights.

To enable faster and efficient data processing in real-time, Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based techniques are employed. AI involves techniques that enable machines or computers to imitate human intelligence with logic, rules, and decision trees. Thus, AI is the superset that involves the concept of machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL). ML is based on AI and is a subset of AI, and has gained significant interest in the modern domain as a core aspect in digitization solutions. Arthur Samuel, one of the pioneers in field of ML, defines it as an area of research that allows machines the potential to learn without being directly programmed [[5](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_005)]. ML helps make data-driven decisions and analyze complex big data by using its powerful and quick processing techniques. DL is a subset of ML that comprises algorithms that train the software to perform various tasks with neural networks [[6](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_006)]. [Figure 8.1](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_001) shows the Venn diagram set which includes AI, ML, and DL.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_001.jpg)

Figure 8.1: Venn diagram for AI, ML, and DL.

In fields involving ML and data mining, SG-based BDA has been researched and implemented for many years. However, the computational resources available to engineers are constrained, considering the computing phenomena called cloud computing (CC). The application of CC is gaining immense importance in the power system point of view as stressed in the General Meetings of International Electrical and Electronics Engineers (IEEE) Power and Energy Society (PES) conducted in 2014 and 2015 [[7](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_007), [8](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_008)]. According to Hilbert and López [[9](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_009)], the world’s effective computational capability, the bidirectional telecommunication bandwidth and globally stored information rise annually by 58%, 28%, and 23%, respectively. Reliable communication, automation, and connectivity are among the key requirements of SG. It facilitates real-time monitoring, analysis and control techniques with simultaneous transfer of information through its reliable communication infrastructure. The distributed automation and connectivity pertaining to the SG is made realistic through the Internet of things (IoT). The network formed by devices, which are interconnected in the system via the Internet following a protocol for sharing data and other information is known as the IoT [[10](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_010)]. There has been an expansion in the usage and development of IoT in recent years, eventually leading to Internet connectivity in various smart devices, thereby reducing cost and optimizing performance [[11](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_011)]. IoT can be implemented in SG across all of its main subsystems – generation, transmission, and distribution [[12](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_012)].

The background of these benefits will contribute to the emergence of SG technology with IoT that is expected to replace the existing conventional SG technology.

For generators, transmission and distribution providers and customers, SG may offer the following advantages.

*   Demand management: The SG facilitates energy usage control for consumers and helps reduce excessive demand and prevent overload scenarios for supply providers.

*   Stability of the network: The SG ensures supply and demand equilibrium and controls voltage levels through various generation and demand control techniques.

*   Reliable network: The SG helps network providers remotely predict and identify faults that can contribute to a rapid electricity failure.

*   Consumer empowerment: The SG delivers real-time utilization, allowing customers to monitor their usage to profit from the off-peak price and reduce their electricity use.

*   Renewable energy sources (RES) integration: SGs enable variable RES to be incorporated by making decisions for energy dispatch and monitoring loads on a real-time basis.

ML and AI can make prompt and accurate decisions in the SG and react to sudden consumer demand shifts, power failures, abrupt changes, and renewable energy production variations or some other significant event. ML is one of the most popular implementations in the SG to anticipate electricity demand and the power generation of unreliable sources. ML can help avoid system failure by evaluating data on equipment failure throughout the industry while leading to asset maintenance and portfolio management. In addition to supervised ML, unsupervised ML may have SG applications, such as developing by clustering of energy demand profiles. The distributed loads perform a major part in influencing the power grid’s frequency when the generation is not able to adjust with the fluctuations in the frequency [[13](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_013)]. In such cases and similar situations, the decisions for energy generation and distribution can employ reinforced learning algorithms to optimize the electricity supply and demand balance. Electric utility practitioners use ML approaches in many core fields in the SG operation. These ML tools are commonly used in: (a) the introduction of renewable energy [[14](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_014)], (b) modeling and estimation techniques [[15](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_015)], (c) tracking power quality events [[16](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_016)], (d) economic optimization for prosumer node [[17](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_017)], (e) managing residential demand for economical optimization [[18](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_018)], (f) consumer energy utilization and prediction technologies [[19](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_019)], (g) islanding identification, (h) maximizing stability for unplanned islanding [[20](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_020)], and many more. As IoT-integrated SG networks build a dynamic, interconnected network and a vast amount of data mostly processed in cloud storage, violation of data privacy is a serious concern [[9](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_009)]. The security risk associated with the power system is comprehensive and extreme when either the demand or supply terminals or both are affected [[21](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_021), [22](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_022)]. Such cyberattacks on SG networks may be conducted to destroy its crucial components, acquire leverage or dominance in its manipulation, financial disruption or compromise power. A layered solution, and technological implementation and policy guidance contribute to the systematic security of the SG.

Technology that can identify and compare attributes of unstructured data with measured data offers many new perspectives. Ultimately, discoveries facilitate the development of new insights for developers and often provide better operational guidance, which is the real outcome of technology powered by BDA. The paper discusses numerous ML approaches that can be implemented in order to maintain the stability, consistency, protection, performance, and sensitivity of the SG. The remainder of this chapter is structured as in [Figure 8.2](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_002).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_002.jpg)

Figure 8.2: Chapter organization.

## 8.2 Big data platform: framework

The electrical generation industry faces tremendous challenges due to the exceptionally high volume and frequency of measurement data. According to the Navigant research analysis, the projected deployed smart meter count would reach 1.1 billion by 2022 [[23](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_023)]. Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI) usually gathers energy consumption data within 15 min to 1 h. Usually, a Phasor Measurement Unit (PMU) samples alternating voltage and current waveforms at very high sampling rates. A phasor data concentrator (PDC) gathers measurement data from a hundred PMUs at a sampling frequency of 30 Hz generates daily data of around 50 GB [[24](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_024)]. BDA presents the utility sector with a range of tools considered to address these problems. The in-memory computation system and parallel computing frameworks such as Hadoop or MapReduce and Spark are proficient in handling a broad range of datasets. Stream processing platforms such as Storm, Streams, and Spark Streaming, are built to interpret data in real time and take necessary actions instantly. Big data can be utilized to enhance both short-term power plant activities and carry out effective planning processes on a long-term basis. They can effectively identify energy theft, identify distribution system topology, assess risks related to asset management, consumer segmentation and targeting, acquire the revenue flow, forecast the load, forecast sustainable generation, etc. [[25](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_025)]. Big data framework requires data aggregation, warehouse management, analytics, and integrates SG demand to establish a number of platforms with outstanding computational capability and respond to different requirements.

The core features [[26](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_026)] of selecting a big data framework are based on

*   Programming model

*   Programming language

*   Data source type

*   Integration of framework with existing machine learning libraries

*   Fault tolerance approach

For selecting the big data framework, the characteristics of the monitored data are also to be taken into account. Considering these characteristics, a big data framework is designed and depending on the application and characteristics, the optimal framework will change. A big data framework chosen for SG may not be effective or suitable for a big data framework used for some other application such as social media or health monitoring purposes. [Table 8.2](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_002) shows the different platforms used at different stages in the SG-based big data framework on a hierarchical basis.

Table 8.2:Different platforms of smart grid)–based big data framework.

| Property | Platform |
| --- | --- |
| Data generation | PMU, AMI, Sensors, SCADA, etc. |
| Data acquisition | Flume |
| Distributed data storing and processing | HDFS, MapReduce, YARN, Cloudera, Hortonworks |
| Data querying | IMPALA, HIVE, Apache Pig |
| Data analytics | Tableau, Mahout, SAMOA |

In the absence of the framework, there is probable redundancy in the processed records, creating overlap against the working applications and misunderstanding over the responsibility of various sections handling the big data. Thus, the framework has to hierarchically define the big data application gaps and define solutions to integrate them. The hierarchical cycle starts with the generation of SG data and concludes with data analytics. The main stages are addressed in these sub-[sections.](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_s_008_s_002_s_001)

### 8.2.1 Data generation

The traditional SCADA system was employed satisfactorily until the blackout in 2003, which hit the north-east part of the United States and the central part of the Canadian grid and lasted for almost a week before it was restored [[27](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_027)]. The post-fault analysis stated that the blackout occurred due to the lack of real-time monitoring of the power system. Therefore, a need was felt for developing a real-time monitoring system and subsequently for the real-time protection and control of the power system. This led to the development of PMU which gives the phasor information of the voltage and current signals and the frequency. The measured data can be transmitted to geographically far apart locations through Global Positioning Satellite (GPS) system to a control center or a central location known as PDC with a time tag associated with it. This time stamping synchronizes the PMU data collected from different installations by providing them a common reference for sampling. The PMUs are dispersed along the grid, placed at various substations at various voltage levels, and other monitoring devices and sensors. This combined system is known popularly as wide area monitoring system (WAMS).

Among the five Vs as essential features of big data pertaining to the power grid transition to the SG, the most important are volume and velocity. SG monitoring data is produced from a wide range of smart meters, collected every few seconds [[28](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_028)]. The data is generated from private houses, office buildings, manufacturing plants, solar farms, wind generators, and numerous power stations. Thus, the obtained data can belong to the generation side, transmission side or the consumer side. The data is gathered from many different sources in the service provider side including smart meters, AMI, sensors, and PMUs. Additionally, environmental factors which include weather data from meteorological centers may also be influential. Considering the data from such sources, the efficiency of the system improves as technological advancements continue to penetrate the whole SG, from generation side to distribution side. Apart from the data created by conventional measurement devices, there are many modern data generating entities such as the electric vehicle (EV) technology. It produces different kinds of data that can be used for numerous pattern analysis and prediction works in optimizing resource utilization. In [[29](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_029)], a mathematical model for developing the traffic pattern and estimating the power for charging the EVs is proposed. [Figure 8.3](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_003) shows the summary of the sources of big data associated with the SG.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_003.jpg)

Figure 8.3: Sources of big data in SG.

### 8.2.2 Data acquisition

SG data acquisition can be split into three different stages; data extraction, data transmission, and pre-processing. Centralized devices proactively gather data that have been produced in the SG. The obtained data is then sent to a cluster master node such as a Hadoop-based master node. After the raw data is processed, it is transmitted to a data storage system for analysis purposes. Given the complex data set, the data obtained can have various formats and details, necessitating its pre-processing. Data integration strategies seek to integrate data from various sources to provide a unified data view [[30](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_030)]. The measured dataset may comprise details which includes time stamp, smart meter references, power generation or consumption information, the geographical location of the sensor, etc. Often, in data pre-processing, incorrect and missing data are reformed or omitted to enhance data accuracy. The outliers are processed by limiting them to either the lower or upper thresholds. The missing data can be handled by using various imputation techniques. An example for a distributed framework that efficiently captures, aggregates, and transmits vast volumes of data from various sources to centralized storage is Flume [[31](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_031)], developed by Apache.

### 8.2.3 Data storage and processing

Once the SG data is obtained, it has to be efficiently processed to bring out the insights that help develop a dynamic picture of the SG. This brings out the hidden patterns, detect correlations in the data and obtain trends that anticipate potential control actions from the massive measurement datasets. Thus, it can be stated that data visualization acts as the link between the raw measurement data and the corresponding control actions which are to be taken in the SG. Hadoop [[32](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_032)] is an open-source platform used to store and process the big data. Rather than storing and processing data in dedicated software, that is costly, it parallels the storage of massive volumes of data on standardized servers. Hadoop’s foundation comprises of two major modules: the storage segment named Hadoop’s Distributed File System (HDFS) [[32](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_032)] and MapReduce [[33](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_033)] which is the processor. Hadoop is regarded as a significant component of big data infrastructure. HDFS is a designed to operate on Google’s File System (GFS) architecture referenced commodity hardware. An HDFS cluster comprises a sole Name Node that handles metadata of the file system, and various data storage nodes. The file is divided into one or even more blocks and placed in a sequence of data nodes. Here, the SG data collected is fragmented into many different frames and then the fragments are allocated to data nodes. Every block undergoes many reconstructions in separate data nodes to prevent missing data. MapReduce [[33](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_033)], Hadoop’s computing tool, comprises a sole master and slave machine, running for each cluster node. Hadoop’s popular open-source releases include Cloudera, MapR, and Hortonworks. National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has established and released the SG Interoperability Standards Framework and Roadmap, Release 1.0, to establish a foundation for prioritizing, organizing, and promoting the implementation of requirements in private sector standards-setting bodies, including foreign standards development organizations similar to the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC) and IEEE [[34](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_034)]. To reliably maintain and run the SG, the big data gathered from the service networks must be evaluated to align electricity production and usage. To reliably forecast potential revenue based on sales results, the BDA model with AI-based ML techniques is essential. [Figure 8.4](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_004) shows the chronological development of the data processing technologies in SG [[35](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_035)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_004.jpg)

Figure 8.4: Chronological development of data processing technologies in SG.

### 8.2.4 Data querying and analytic components

The broad array of data from electrical sources and other nonelectrical sources has resulted in the formation of big data in the SG. The measured SG data should be transmitted dynamically to increase SG performance. This data is analyzed to provide suggestive or feedback measures for the power system operator, for researchers to validate data mining and correlation algorithms and for customers to visualize and acquire an awareness of their consumption profiles. The SG allows incorporation of instant reports to device managers from sensors mounted at power stations, transformers, and load centers. Utilities already have resources to help execute grid functions that were actually not available before, or rather complicated. By combining output characteristics and load statistics, operators may understand consumer’s load and usage to ensure that resources stay within the allowable limit over time. However, sharing of SG data must be weighed against data protection issues. Operational decision making involves a continuous sequence of analytical processing, such as a given range of power system faults or events, making the optimized decision and monitoring the consequence, impact assessment of the action taken, and corresponding dynamic tuning of model parameters. Demand-response (DR) technologies have effective options for increasing the reliability of the SG [[36](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_036)]. In [[37](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_037)], a real-time algorithm based on DR is developed that offers optimum control of load devices in a system. The energy management center (EMC) computes the retail prices and the device’s purchase energy [[37](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_037)]. The general outline of adaptive analytics used in SG is demonstrated in [Figure 8.5](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_005).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_005.jpg)

Figure 8.5: Adaptive analytics in SG.

## 8.3 Machine learning and artificial intelligence approach to big data analytics in smart grid

Recent advancements in measurement and monitoring technologies significantly led to the increase in the volume, variety, and velocity of measured data in the transmission and distribution side of the power system. The development of high sampling rate devices such as PMU has given power system operators and service providers a broad insight to high-quality knowledge of the power system that would have been non-observable through conventional data collection approaches such as SCADA. The PMUs provide dynamic measurements of the voltage and current phasors for all the three phases, frequency and rate of change of frequency with high precision and improved sampling rate [[38](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_038)]. Event detection [[39](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_039)], oscillation monitoring [[40](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_040)], state estimation [[41](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_041)], topology detection [[42](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_042)], and so on are some of the major applications that were made realistic due to the presence of PMU data. The artificial neural network (ANN), genetic algorithm (GA), reinforcement learning (RL), and multi-agent systems are some of the standard AI methods to address classification, prediction, networking, optimization, and monitoring strategies [[43](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_043)]. The lack of sophisticated automated controllable tools has led to many device operations being conducted manually or at a basic automation stage. Having AI in the grid structure will bring technologies and offer the SG innovative approaches to sort these challenges. One such example is the optimization of controllable loads using smart techniques, which leads to cost savings. [Figure 8.6](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_006) illustrates the incorporation of the PMU network, ML-based event prediction techniques and conventional power system infrastructure.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_006.jpg)

Figure 8.6: SG network with ML-based event prediction techniques and conventional power system infrastructure.

Modern power systems usually feature sophisticated networking, system dynamics and monitoring techniques and are sensitive to user decisions. In the future, the incorporation of these smart technologies, interconnected usage trends, improved performance and reliability criteria would transform the traditional power system architecture from a physical framework to a cyber-physical framework that involves innovative modeling, control, monitoring, design and diagnostic methodologies. The power grids would also benefit from open-source platforms and current ML solutions to transform their infrastructure for data-driven management. ML can be interpreted as identifying an undefined function *f*, generated by measurable results. The function aims to collect properties within data describing linearity (or non-linearity). Mathematically it can be represented as

(8.1)*f* : *X* → *Y*, *X* ∈ ℝ^(*n**x**m*), *Y* ∈ ℝ^(*n*);  *n*, *m* ∈ 1, 2, …, *k*

where *X*, *Y* represents the labels for corresponding input and *f* represents the function to which unobservable data is input to obtain a suitable decision. This section addresses ML and data-mining algorithms, processes, and strategies that can be adapted in power systems for research, data processing, regression, and pattern classification. The common ML approaches include supervised, unsupervised and reinforcement learning.

### 8.3.1 Overview of supervised learning approach

In supervised learning, the machine is trained with the help of properly labelled data, and using this data, the machine will predict the output for a given input. The objective of supervised learning is to achieve the output *y* by mapping or correlating it with the input *x*. The observation set or the sample set is represented as (*x*[*i*], *y*[*i*]), *i* = 1, 2, 3, …, *n*, where *x*[*i*] represents the feature and *y*[*i*] represents the labels for the *i*^(*t**h*) sample. Several methodologies were suggested from very different viewpoints to obtain the “optimal” mapping for a function class **H**. One of the widely employed methods is to identify *f* as “regularized empirical risk reduction” problem [[44](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_044)] with the mathematical relation given by

(8.2)$\underset{f \in \mathbf{H}}{min}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{n}L\left( {y_{i},f\left( x_{i} \right)} \right) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\left| |f| \right|_{\mathbf{H}}$

$\underset{f \in \mathbf{H}}{min}\frac{1}{n}\underset{i = 1}{\overset{n}{\Sigma}}L\left( {y_{i},\ f\left( x_{i} \right)} \right)$ provides the efficiency of fitness with the classifier function *f* for a particular loss function, whereas $\frac{\lambda}{2}\left| |f| \right|_{\mathbf{H}}$ represents the regularization term which penalizes the mapping norm in function class **H**.

Two separate learning tasks may be carried out with supervised learning, namely, regression and classification-based learning. For solving regression problems, the input is considered to be a vector of dimension ‘n’, whereas the output *y* ∈ ℝ is considered as set of real numbers. The input *x* ∈ ℝ^(*n*) of classification problems is considered the same as in regression problems, the output is considered as an integer, *y* ∈ ℤ. The development of supervised learning approach follows the stages that involve the training, validation, and testing of data. Some of the most popular supervised learning algorithms are ANN, decision tree, support vector machine (SVM), and k-nearest neighbors (KNN)-based techniques. The loss function *L* relies on the multiple options of the function class *H*. Numerous challenges in learning can be developed accordingly in the norm *k* to perform different duties. [Table 8.3](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_003) lists several samples of popular ML frameworks that suit in this category.

Table 8.3:Different supervised learning techniques employed in smart grid.

| Learning technique | Types or classification | Sub-classification |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Supervised learning | Regression |  
1.  Linear regression

2.  Nonparametric regression

3.  Logistic regression

 |
| Decision tree |  |  |
| Support vector machine |  |  |

[Table 8.4](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_004) summarizes the various supervised learning techniques used in SG.

Table 8.4:Different supervised learning techniques employed in smart grid.

| Reference | Principle used | Purpose served |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [[45](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_045)] | Clustering | Event classification |
| [[46](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_046)] | Multivariable regression model | Track fault locations |
| [[47](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_047)] | Clustering | Identification of inter-area oscillations |
| [[48](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_048)] | Pattern recognition and linear discrimination | Identification of fault location |
