### 8.3.2 Overview of unsupervised learning approach

In supervised learning systems, there might be instances where the availability of labelled data is restricted due to limitation in the number of sensors or due to the lack of expertise in this field. Hence, it is the machine’s task to obtain the hidden pattern and related insights. In certain implementations, an alternative learning method, termed unsupervised learning, allows the algorithm to learn valuable function features within the dataset. One of the most common methodologies used for unsupervised learning today is reinforcement learning (RL), which was initiated to overcome stochastic optimal control issues in the power grid [[55](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_055)]. RL methods are widely found in diverse applications such as system control [[56](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_056)] and transfer learning [[57](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_057)]. The benefit of integrating reinforcement learning with transfer-learning methods is evident. Therefore, the transfer of knowledge from a global to a local viewpoint is preferred by researchers in order to improvise energy demand volatility. Clustering, feature extraction, and reconstruction are three key objects that can be achieved by unsupervised learning.

#### 8.3.2.1 Selection of appropriate model

Typical predictive analysis or machine learning techniques train and forecast data by matching (in a general sense) parametric or nonparametric models. There is, however, no paradigm uniformly appropriate for data and targets. An inappropriate model or method selection may lead to wrong findings, assumptions or misleading forecasting results. Thus, a key move in traditional data analysis is to evaluate a collection of candidate models (the model class) and then choose the most suitable one. In other terms, model selection includes selecting a statistical model from a model class, and providing a collection of data [[58](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_058)]. A model can be represented as *M*[*m*] = {*p*[*θ*[*m*]] : *θ*[*m*] ∈ *H*[*m*]} which is a set of probability density functions to denote the data set *z*[1], *z*[2], … *z*[*n*]. *H*[*m*] represents the parameter space which is linked with *M*[*m*]. The model class *M*[*m*], where *m* represents the set of models indexed by *m* ∈ 𝕄. The cardinality of 𝕄 is selected based on the size of the sample, *n*. *d*[*m*] represents the dimension of the parameter represented in the model. Its maximum log-likelihood value is represented in (8.3) as

(8.3)*l*[*n*, *m*](*θ̂*[*m*])  *o**r*  *l̂*[*n*, *m*],   *w**h**e**r**e* *θ̂*[*m*] = *a**r**g**m**a* *x*[*θ*[*m*] ∈ *H*[*m*]]*p*[*θ*[*m*]](*z*[1], …, *z*[*n*])

For the parametric framework, there exists some *m* ∈ *M* and some value of *θ*2[*] ∈ *H*[*m*], such that *p** = *p*[*θ**] where *p** is the true data generating distribution compared to the true data generating distribution. For non – parametric framework, *p** is not considered for the model class.

#### 8.3.2.2 Feature selection

One of the fundamental concepts for the efficiency of any data analytic system is recognizing the most insightful data trends or functions – feature selection (FS). With current advances in technology, power system studies currently have access to real-time sensor data, demand and consumption statistics and other relevant information. In many instances, original data sets are in extremely diverse formats, such as time series, independent databases, polls, etc., which have to be formatted to derive timely features for trend identification or diagnostic applications. Such features align with the targeted application, while others are less pertinent or obsolete and ignored. Most ML approaches will not benefit their output if they use meaningless and repetitive patterns as the input. Therefore, the purpose of FS is to classify the utmost insightful attribute from the collected data. The ultimate data processing method in the terminology of data mining for ML is named feature engineering. The techniques used for FS are mainly classified into the filter, wrapper and embedded methods [[59](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_059)]. The filter method extracts the most predominant aspects and filters out the remainder, irrespective of the expectation of the underlying model. Selected objectives are certain insightful measurements that display the effectiveness of a feature subset. Hilbert-Schmidt Independence Criterion (HSIC), t-statistics, signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) are some of the commonly used filter method–based techniques. In wrapper method, the error pertaining to the prediction process is reduced by training the model and testing the function set by choosing the predicted general output. Including sparseness in the training model development, embedded method selects function subsets implicitly. The key principle is that the risk minimization could include sparsity limits or penalties. [Table 8.5](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_005) lists the various methods used in feature selection and their comparison.

### 8.3.3 Classification problem

Of late, various ML-based techniques have been formulated to detect whether the power system is operating under the abnormal condition and finally classify the corresponding condition based on known events. An aspect that raises the complexity of handling power systems is monitoring data sources with time-series progressions. This problem is usually addressed by extracting the features prior to the actual classification stage. Extraction of features involves converting the insightful output of the data involving time series into scalar quantities. Such a technique consumes more time as well as requires the intervention of software expertise to prevent the loss of data in the process. However, features collected to detect certain system events may not be insightful under unforeseen circumstances contributing to low monitoring results. Time series classification techniques are classified into feature-based and distance-based techniques.

#### 8.3.3.1 Feature-based (FB) time series classification

Prior to the actual classification stage, FB methods conduct the feature extraction procedure. A moveable window *k* of specific length *n* is generally taken into consideration to achieve a time series *z*[*k*] with the original signal v(t) and a range *x* of *p* characteristics is computed on the same signal. The frequently selected parameters are variance, mean, maxima, minima, and so on, all of which apply to time series. The concept behind these approaches is to collect statistics of signals and classify a particular category of signals. In principle, employing a second-order statistic is adequate to characterize the signal for a weakly stationary process; nevertheless, signals extracted from real-time situations are not stationary due to multiple disturbance factors and several functionalities might be required for the insightful content to be summarized [[69](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_069)]. The learning law is constructed by defining a series of *N* observations along with a design matrix *D* when considering the learning process in FB methods.

(8.4)$D = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{x}^{(1)} & y^{(1)} \\ \mathbf{x}^{(2)} & y^{(2)} \\ . & . \\ . & . \\ . & . \\ x^{(N)} & y^{(N)} \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times {({p + 1})}}\,$

Random forest (RF) and SVM follow the FB method of classification. [Table 8.6](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_006) lists the various SG-based applications that use FB time series calculations.

Table 8.6:Various smart grid FB time series classification.

| Reference | FB method | Data source applied | Application |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [[70](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_070)] | Discrete wavelet transform (DWT), neural networks | PMU | Theft detection |
| [[71](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_071)] | Decision tree, SVM | AMI | Event and fault detection, isolation |
| [[72](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_072)] | Hidden Markov models (HMM) | PMU | Fault detection |
| [[73](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_073)] | Clustering, outlier detection, FB Meteo, recursive feature elimination | Meteo | Prediction |
| [[74](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_074)] | Discrete Fourier transform (DFT), DWT, fast discrete s-transform (FDST) | PMU | Fault detection and isolation |
| [[75](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_075)] | Semi-supervised SVM, multiple kernel learning | PMU | Anomaly detection |

#### 8.3.3.2 Distance-based (DB) time series classification

DB methods skip the stage of feature extraction based on the principle of suitable distances. This approach has been inspired by the possibility that it could be time-consuming to extract the feature and the information based on the original signal could be considered lost [[76](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_076)]. Consequently, due to disturbance, a direct DB time-series comparison will result in undesirable significant issues and performance. Thus, careful selection of the distance parameters, distinguishing between the complexity of the measurement and precision in the classification is necessary. Primary methods using DB classification include the dynamic time warping (DTW) technique. DB classification techniques are further subdivided into parametric DB, pure DB, and reduction-based DB. [Table 8.7](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_007) lists the various SG-based applications that use FB time series calculations.

Table 8.7:Various smart grid DB time series classification.

| Reference | DB method | Data source applied | Application |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [[70](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_070)] | DTW, modified dynamic time warping (MDTW) | AMI, AM | Novelty detection |
| [[77](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_077)] | Wavelet | PMU | Anomaly and event detection |
| [[78](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_078)] | One class classifier | Smart sensors | Detection and isolation of faults |
| [[79](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_079)] | Iterative Hilbert Huang transform | PMU | Monitoring |
| [[80](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_080)] | DWT | PMU | Detection of faults |
| [[81](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_081)] | DWT | PMU | Detection and isolation of faults |

## 8.4 Cloud computing and Internet of things

Big data from different parts of the power grid, namely generation, transmission and distribution must be processed safely, accurately, economically and promptly. CC infrastructure serves a significant role in processing and storing big data [[82](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_082)]. To obtain an understanding of the relationship between CC and big data, CC may be viewed as an upper-level elemental framework for big data activity. CC is a platform that transforms the information infrastructure and guarantees the big data can be used in decision making. CC is mainly implemented for making use of its enormous computational and storage resources [[83](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_083)]. Simultaneously, cloud system’s rigorous computing and storage technologies support big data analytics. CC is beneficial for BDA as it aims to address their issues related to access and processing, facilitates analytical performance by taking advantage of CC’s parallel computation ability, and enhances big data management utilizing CC-based distributed storage technology.

### 8.4.1 Related computational drawbacks

Within the SG system, the culmination of massive volumes of data poses challenges and opportunities for various monitoring algorithms. Because of the availability of the comprehensive data set from numerous sensors and smart devices around the SG, the framework can be more apparent, with more significant and precise estimates. However, owing to the inclusion of several active nodes, power system optimization issues become computationally difficult. This section discusses the challenges, requirements, and related topics based on the computational background.

#### 8.4.1.1 Challenges in smart grid: security

The bulk of the big data related to SG are stored with the application of cloud storage techniques. However, the amount of storage capacity and network bandwidth are key constraints of cloud systems when the volume of data that needs to be communicated is expanding. The implementation of smart sensors and meters has posed questions regarding the security in data transmission, especially when huge volumes of customer data are collected for processing. Data hacking can help the intruders give an idea about the power scenario of a geographical area and create unprecedented power system events. Also, large expenditures are already made globally and as a result, appropriate protective requirements must be met to avoid unforeseen damage that could prove to be a threat either to the power grids or operational staff. Hence, it is essential to identify the threats that could happen in the SG and take action accordingly. Thus it is broadly classified into technical and non-technical sources of threats. The technical sources are further classified into infrastructural, operational and data management security issues. The infrastructural part involves the security aspects related to the SG infrastructure such as generators, transformers, metering devices, etc., and against cyberattacks and thefts. Operational security ensures the system data security, qualification of the operating personnel, control actions based on system status, and so on. Data Management security ensures the real-time monitoring and storage of data while ensuring the privacy norms for operators and consumers. Non-technical sources are further classified into environmental security and government policies. [Figure 8.7](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_007) highlights the different classes of threats that are feasible in the SG.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_007.jpg)

Figure 8.7: Classification of sources of SG threats.

Stuxnet, programmed to concentrate on Iran’s industrial power networks, is the first documented intrusion on industrial networks. The probable goal was Nataz’s uranium enrichment facility, where it induced centrifugal overheating by modifying programmable logic controllers, which then collapsed rapidly, rendering the centrifuge’s pretend completely functional [[84](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_084)]. Operation Night Dragon is a 2006 multinational cyberthreat designation where the hackers used typical tactics like SQL injection, brute-force attack, and phishing. After taking control of these computers, the hackers uploaded programming software for network administrators to access devices on the network. These techniques then assisted target company networks to access confidential data [[85](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_085)]. Other popular attacks [[86](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_086)] on the SG include the Shamoon Virus [[87](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_087)], which targeted users of Microsoft Windows NT from Saudi Aramco and BlackEnergy malware, which caused the cyberattack in Ukraine in December 2015 [[88](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_088)]. Inadequate system awareness is considered as the fundamental cause behind the North American blackouts [[89](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_089)]. The significance of cyberattack research is to ensure secure and effective power supply activity. A cyberattack may result in an imbalance that damages the facilities or a misleading demand that can lead to an inappropriate amount of energy generation [[90](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_090)]. Also, a security threat may trigger false alarms such as a fault in the power system. Other SG disturbances, electric vehicle networks, are also likely. In [[91](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_091)], it is seen that malicious attacks by preventing contact with a system will interrupt operations in power station machines. Thus, real-time cyberattack monitoring is critical for the stable functioning of SG. Hence, online and real-time device monitoring is necessary to detect planned cyberattacks and resist them [[92](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_092)].

#### 8.4.1.2 Detection of smart grid attacks

A cyberattacker’s fundamental aim is to disrupt or deceive the smart grid) state estimation process into triggering wide power outages or exploiting energy market prices [[93](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_093)]. The United States Cyber Emergency Response Team, (US-CERT) responded to around 200 cyber-related events across the entire infrastructure sector between October 2012 and May 2013 [[94](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_094)]. It was evident from the report that around 53% of the attacks were targeted at the energy sector, followed by the critical manufacturing sector, which reported 17%. The graphical representation in [Figure 8.8](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_fig_008) shows the division of the attacks which took place in the mentioned period.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-008_fig_008.jpg)

Figure 8.8: Pie-chart showing the attacks reported by US-CERT during October 2012 to May 2013.

There are various forms of cyberattacks, including false data injection (FDI), jamming and access denial attacks. FDI threats introduce deceptive or false data to sensor measurements [[95](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_095)], jamming attempts corrupt meter measurements through adding noise, and access denial threats obstruct device access to meter measurements [[96](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_096)]. The SG is a dynamic network where any device fault or intrusion in a system part may cause a major catastrophe on the whole grid in no time. Quick cyberattack detection is essential in order to ensure an appropriate and efficient response. The fastest change detection framework is really helpful, including identification strategies like Euclidean detector [[97](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_097)] and cosine-similarity metric-based detector [[98](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_098)]. These are generic since they do not require any prototype of the attack to be developed. They measure the variations between real meter readings and predicted readings using estimators such as Kalman filters and identify anomalies that surpass a defined threshold variation. These detectors do not recognize temporal relationships between targeted or outlier measurements and generate judgments for each sample. Therefore, they cannot differentiate abrupt and impulsive random noise generated from persistent irregularities such as system interferences. Therefore, more accurate universal attack detection schemes are required [[99](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_099)]. The Ukrainian power grid was attacked on December 23, 2015, and the subsequent power outage impacted about 2 lakh citizens over many hours [[100](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_100)]. Traditional detectors identify a sample as anomalous if it reaches a certain threshold [[101](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_101)]. More modern methods are often considered for classifying outlier measurements [[102](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_102)]. Besides, a Markov graph framework [[103](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_103)] for state variables is first trained under the normal working condition of the system and then the anomalous behavior is identified using the comparison of new steps to the existing nominal model. Furthermore, a multistep method is provided in [[104](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_104)], focused on the least square (LS) state estimator, to detect cyber-related attacks on the measurements from sensors, system parameters, and network topology, thus improving threat protection. Kalman filters [[105](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_105)] are employed for the FDI attack detection and extended Kalman filters [[106](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_106)] to bind the impact of outliers in the state estimation process.

### 8.4.2 Cybersecurity: state and scope of threats

When SCADA systems were introduced years before, the main challenge was the structural disruption of the utility’s infrastructure as they used independent and committed networks protected by conventional air-gapped separations [[107](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_107)]. Over the last few years, SCADA networks were installed with IoT modules that often connect over local networks exposing multiple loopholes and cyberattacks [[108](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_108)]. The Industrial Control Systems Cyber Emergency Response Team (ICS-CERT) conveyed in 2014 that out of 245 events detected, 79 were identified as critical infrastructure events aimed at the power sector [[109](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_109)]. Based on interests and intentions, SCADA attacks and targets may be classified as [[110](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_110)]

1.  Internal malicious attacks – Personnel, workers or consultants knowingly causing hazards

2.  Internal non-malicious attacks – Workers making an unintended error that would harm power plant assets

3.  External opportunistic attacks – Hackers challenging or messing around

4.  External deliberate attacks – An external group exploiting weaknesses in another country or state’s power system

Substantial advancement concerning the obtaining of reliable measurement data has been achieved by installing PMU devices in the power systems, as they are time-synchronized, thus improving rapid decision-making and control that eventually helps to build a pathway for the practical realization of Dynamic Security Assessment (DSA) [[111](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_111)]. In view of constant blackouts and other power grid challenges, all energy suppliers have begun to realize the need for efficient, well-equipped, quick, effective and advanced protection and stability methodologies for the SG. In cyber-physical systems (CPS), networks with multifunctional smart sensors and actuators are provided with bidirectional communication and computing capabilities that measure, process and communicate the data collected to prevent cyberattacks. The cyber-related attacks on CPS can be either denial of service (DoS) attacks or deception attacks. In the last thirty years, ML algorithms have proven highly effective in power system protection and security. The proposed SVM method in [[112](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_112)] is used for handling big data for addressing concerns related to protection and is tested on the Microsoft malware platform. The outcomes are effective, but the absence of adequate IoT applications to secure private and sensitive IoT component data from cybersecurity threats. Blockchain technology [[113](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_113), [114](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_114)] offers a secure, integrated and secure framework for IoT applications in decentralized applications such as SG. As a consequence of this modern technology, consumers could feel challenged as their electricity supplier may potentially gain access to user information in the distribution side. Meanwhile, the energy suppliers such as electric utilities and power grid operators are forced to upgrade the generation and run the power plants more effectively especially by efficiently integrating decentralized and green renewable energy sources. Even though multiple instances of use cases depend extensively on consumer information and private data in the SG [[115](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_115)], strong consumer engagement, and a high level of consumer support are crucial. Through offering a reasonable degree of anonymity, which in effect is accomplished by ensuring proven protected technology and standards, such as authentication and encryption, acceptability may be accomplished. Research and assessment are essential to implement privacy enhancing technology (PETs), improving consumer awareness and applying potential usage cases [[116](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_116)]. Apart from the traditional CPS of the SG, new innovations such as the EV technology often form separate cyber-physical networks. Several EVs are connected to the grid to share and optimize the power accessible from the grid with the load. The aspect of vehicle-to-grid (V2G) would present a cyber-physical structure, with a physical component integrated with a communication and measurement part, the combination of which forms a CPS [[29](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_029)].

## 8.5 Big data analytics: applications for smart grid

Accurate power system fault and event identification are important to maintain the efficient functioning of the power grid. In the conventional grid, multiple sensors are mounted and tracking devices are analyzed. However, due to reported data complexities, early flaws or errors cannot be easily visualized. Numerous guidelines were suggested to direct the advancement of big data in SGs. Following the 2007 regulatory framework of the initial conventional description for SGs as per the Energy Independence and Security Act (EISA-2007) [[117](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_117)], standardization attempts for SGs communication and data sharing commenced. Several standardization organizations, including IEEE, International Organization for Standardization/International Electrotechnical Commission (ISO/IEC), NIST, ITU-T, and so on have steadily evolved and accomplished the standardization process. The IEEE and NIST standards are generalist in nature whereas IEC and ITU-T’s standards and specific to big data challenges in smart grid. The relevant standards related to big data analytics are listed in [Table 8.8](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_008).

### 8.5.1 Detection of faults

Generally, a power system fault occurs when one or more parameters are unstable or where a surge or fault occurs due to the failure of insulation system, operator error or structural damage [[120](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_120)]. Maintaining the stability of the power system is one of the most significant issues, since failure of the power system will result in blackouts which affects the whole functioning of the society with a significant loss of revenue. Therefore, the prevention of such catastrophic events is important, for which the smart grid requires an efficient fault detection and recognition system. Fault detection based on system line tracking technique is built in [[121](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_121)] relying on the angle of phasor and system topology details, which is also established for double-line outage detection in [[122](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_122)]. A model is designed to replicate the worst-case power grid configuration based on linear programming algorithm to forecast power grid vulnerabilities [[123](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_123)]. In [[124](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_124)], a Petri network-based fault detection and identification approach is recommended to capture the distribution network’s security mechanism’s modelling details. If a smart grid fault is located and identified, the subsequent phase is to establish its position and the region impacted by it before taking the appropriate control strategies. Fault location identification based on the Gaussian Markov random field is suggested to use phasor angle measurements at the smart gridbuses [[125](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_125)]. A wavelet-based approach is developed to identify and locate faults in [[126](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_126)]. In power systems, fault prediction, diagnosis and location are typically solved as a single problem. A multifunctional approach may be accepted as having a wide range of solutions implemented in smart grid systems, including detecting, identifying and locating a fault. Recently, research on smart grid–based ML techniques [[127](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_127)] has progressed data-driven methods to predict and detect power system faults.

### 8.5.2 Demand side and power generation management

SG operators depend intensely on energy demand forecasts to balance the generation and demand, thus preventing the power grid from getting overloaded. Introducing RES and distributed energy sources renders it more challenging to balance the power demand and the electricity generation. Presently, estimating both energy demand and distributed generation is vital to recognize how much output conventional generators require to satisfy the current demand. Load modelling and prediction are exceptional uses of big data and can be categorized based on the length of the period as long, medium, and short terms [[128](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_128)]. The long- and medium-term forecasting techniques are used to enhance the power system planning capability, whereas the short term techniques are used for power distribution, pricing, and demand response. Numerous load forecasting analyses are done focused on big data analytics. CC applications in demand side management (DSM) and DR in SG implementations are analyzed in [[129](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_129)]. A comprehensive approach for analyzing the SG forecasting model focused on big data analytics is provided in [[130](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_130)]. Some of the recent techniques used for demand and generation forecasting and management are tabulated in [Table 8.9](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_009).

Table 8.9:Demand side and power generation management in smart grid.

| [[132](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_132)] | Energy load forecasting | Empirical mode decomposition |
| [[133](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_133), [134](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_134), [135](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_135)] | Short-term load forecasting and load classification | Clustering |

### 8.5.3 Demand response and dynamic pricing

To integrate demand response, it is essential to consider and estimate energy flexibility on the consumer side. Simultaneously, the new smart meter apps with the help of non-intrusive load monitoring (NILM) help easy tracking of customer power consumption levels in real time and evaluate customer consumption behavior. By utilizing NILM, the instruments that are used and their specific utilization may be calculated. [Table 8.10](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_010) summarizes the various techniques used in SG for incorporating demand response.

Table 8.10:Different techniques used in smart grid for incorporating demand response.

| Reference | Principle used | Purpose served |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [[139](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_139)] | Deep learning | Identifying load flexibility |
| [[140](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_140)] | RNN | Classify consumers based on the consumption |
| [[141](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_141)] | Factored Boltzmann machines | Identify and predict demand flexibility on real-time basis |
| [[142](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_142)] | Reinforcement learning | Cluster consumers based on willingness to enforce restrictions |

Dynamic pricing is a crucial method to involve consumers in demand response and electricity exchange. In reality, due to the lack of knowledge on the time-varying demand and energy usage habits of consumers and the instability of costs on the supply sector, dynamic pricing is extremely difficult. [Table 8.11](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_tab_011) summarizes the various techniques employed in SG for implementing dynamic pricing.

Table 8.11:Different techniques used in smart grid for implementing dynamic pricing.

| Reference | Principle used | Purpose served |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [[143](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_143)] | Reinforcement learning | Dynamic pricing without knowledge of system dynamics |
| [[144](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_144)] | Reinforcement learning | DR with automatic generation control |
| [[145](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_145)] | Reinforcement learning | Constrained energy trading between suppliers |
| [[146](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_146)] | Q-Learning | Developing tariff strategies for agents in SG market |

## 8.6 Electric vehicles

BDA is expected to handle the growing challenges emerging due to the increased practice of adopting EVs, reflecting the modern transport services [[147](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_147)]. Increased acceptance of EVs is regarded as a mechanism for decreasing *C**O*[2] pollution also. In general, EVs are designed to boost the grid by storing and transferring electricity to other networks, thus optimizing energy usage from variable RES and enabling users to recharge their vehicles in various locations. The reliability assessment and EV status issues [[147](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_147)], driving behavior [[148](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_148)], energy consumption and management [[149](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_149)], and battery power are the focuses in this context, as per the research works conducted. Managing EV activities would require methodologies that can address problems affecting a wide variety of heterogeneous groups and individuals, such as vehicle owners, charging point owners, and supply providers, each having different priorities, desires, and benefits [[150](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_150)]. Some of the AI approaches involve energy-efficient routing of EV and maximizing the energy harvested during the travel [[151](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_151)], congestion management [[152](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_152)], and integrating EVs to the SG [[153](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_153)].

### 8.6.1 Power system monitoring, control, protection, and management

Recently, there has been an emerging question about how to manage the integration of clean energy sources into SG [[159](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_159), [160](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_160), [161](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_161), [162](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_162)]. To ensure stable service, the incorporation of renewable sources of energy is vital; accordingly, power system control can be used to achieve versatile activity in the SG [[163](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_163), [164](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_164)]. In order to provide protection in different operations of the SG, transmission systems operators (TSOs) should be able to collect accurate data of the condition of the grid. This is ensured through the data measurement sources, out of which the primary data source for SG are the AMI readings [[154](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_154)]. SG data collected from various measurement devices such as PMUs, sensor units, smart meters, and other IEDs is regarded as input signals which are employed to develop insights for improving customer support, predictive modeling, fault detection and diagnosis and outages, fraud and maintaining and enhancing cybersecurity [[155](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_155)]. Smart Grid Monitoring includes detection of events and estimation of the dynamic state dependent on AMI, PMU and smart devices [[156](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_156)], wide area situational awareness [[155](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_155)], islanding detection [[157](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_157)], oscillation monitoring [[40](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_040)], dynamic rotor angle analysis [[158](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_158)], and asset management [[156](#9783110713794_020436.xhtml_b_9783110714043-008_ref_156)] are also enhanced with BDA.

## 8.7 Summary

With the use of modern measuring devices in power systems, a vast amount of the data is available at the monitoring side. But, a major part of the data has not yet utilized properly. Many of the tools that are utilized for measuring and decision making still use traditional algorithms such as data warehousing. However, with the novel BDA, some of the core concepts, like cleansing, dimension reduction, etc., have been reviewed. Eventually, we recognize many obstacles and measures which need to be taken in order for grid operators to be adequately equipped for relying on BDA. A comprehensive analysis was presented in this chapter about the state-of-the-art artificial intelligence strategies designed to help numerous SG applications, including RES integration, energy storage systems integration, DR management, cybersecurity, etc. These approaches are supposed to boost efficiency further and simplify SG operations. SG will progress further by implementing automated technologies for the entire power system – ranging from the generation side to the distribution side, including the maintenance activities. Even recently, most activities are performed manually or with a basic degree of automation. The introduction of distributed automation systems helps in fault detection and healing, economic optimization, outage management, voltage regulation, activation of protective equipment and DER integration. Cybersecurity guidelines would introduce potential to ML, information theory and knowledge-based detection applications. In summary, implementing AI strategies may be utilized to bring down energy losses in the distribution network to improve energy efficiency. AI techniques will also enable enhanced and centralized control of distributed energy, improving SG technologies to further improve the SG. Whether the SG gains through innovative and upgraded technologies is specifically related to data acquisition, storage and analysis stages. This chapter has discussed BDA, addressing its relevance to the SG, focusing on challenges related to machine learning and artificial intelligence aspects and the relevance of cyber-physical security and safety.

# 9 IoT-based intelligent solar energy-harvesting technique with improved efficiency

Emdadul HoqueDepartment of Mechanical Engineering, Rajshahi University of Engineering and Technology, Rajshahi, BangladeshDip Kumar SahaDepartment of Mechatronics Engineering, Rajshahi University of Engineering and Technology, Rajshahi, BangladeshDibakar RakshitCenter for Energy Studies, Indian Institute of Technology, Delhi, India

**Acknowledgment:** The authors would like to acknowledge the heartiest gratitude to the Department of Mechanical Engineering, Rajshahi University of Engineering and Technology (RUET), for the support to continue research in the field of renewable energy technology.

#### Abstract

Due to the immense development of the Internet and artificial intelligence technology, Internet of things (IoT) and machine learning in green energy monitoring has become a topic of interest for researchers. In this chapter, an intelligent solar energy-harvesting technique is presented. The efficiency of the solar power generation is heavily influenced by the surface temperature of the solar photovoltaics (PV). After a certain temperature threshold, the efficiency of solar PV decreases. It can be monitored with the help of a thermal camera operated by a drone. The thermal image captured is sent to a cloud server using a NodeMCU (microcontroller unit) module through IoT. Captured data can be analyzed from a distant location. A drone generally covered a vast region, including the outside boundary of the solar panel. A convolutional neural network can be used to extract accurate surface temperature from the solar panel. Based on these data, an active cooling system can automatically control the solar PV’s surface temperature. The proposed method also includes an automated solar monitoring system to obtain better efficiency. The combination of state-of-the-art cooling, intelligent solar tracking with IoT-based condition monitoring of solar panels has made the proposed method interesting for energy researchers.

Keywords: solar tracking, green energy, machine learning, active cooling,

## 9.1 Introduction

With rapid technological development and improvement of people’s socioeconomic conditions, home appliance usage has also increased over the last few years. There was a time when only some privileged people used washing machines and air-conditioner-like products. But now, it becomes a daily need even for middle-class people. The ultimate effect is the increase in electricity consumption. The rapid decrease of conventional energy sources also exacerbates this situation. The challenge of finite fossil fuel supplies, as well as the harmful effects of traditional energy sources, has prompted a quest for more environmentally friendly energy sources [[1](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_001)]. To meet the cumulative increase in power demand, we have no other way to go for green energy technologies. Solar energy, wind energy, hydroenergy, tidal energy, biomass energy and geothermal energy are typical sources of green energy [[2](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_002)]. Solar energy is the most widely used green energy source of these. Due to its eco-friendly behavior, it has got an enormous response from the researchers. Although solar panel absorbs 80% of incident ration, only a limited portion is converted into useful electrical energy [[3](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_003)]. The majority of the incoming radiation is transformed into heat. The performance of solar panels highly depends on surface temperature. Higher cell temperature is responsible for lower conversion efficiency and lesser sustainability over the period.

The surface temperature of a photovoltaic (PV) solar panel rises to 50–60 °C during the summer months as ambient temperatures rise and reaches 80 °C that prevents delivering its optimal output [[4](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_004)]. Active cooling technologies, including water cooling and fan cooling, improve solar panel efficiencies by monitoring and maintaining cell temperatures [[5](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_005)]. A minimizing reflection technique with a thin water film (1 mm) was used by Krauter [[6](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_006)], which runs across the panel’s face. The electrical performance increased with better optics and cell temperatures for the day, 10.3% (8.9% after counting the energy dissipated to run pump). Saad and Masud [[7](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_007)] managed cooling the PV array and increased device efficiency by about 15% at the highest radiation conditions by installing water trickling setup on the grid’s upper surface. Abdolzadeh and Ameri [[8](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_008)] increased the operation of a PV water pumping system via pouring water on the front of PV cells as a result of decreasing temperature difference and cell reflection. The findings revealed that the strength of the cells improved due to water spraying.

PV devices are now increasingly being deployed all over the world. However, a fixed flat-plate device is usually used in installations, utilizing pre-specified angles obtained through geographical positioning. Thus, due to the variance in solar energy in the advancement of a day and the periods in a year, the power produced by PV systems drops rapidly. Chaowanan et al. [[9](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_009)] proposed a low-cost, automated double-axis solar tracking system to maximize solar production. The monitoring method rotates along the significant (north–south) and minor (east–west) axes using the sensors’ digital logic in a pseudo-azimuthal system.

With immense progress due to the fourth industrial revolution Internet of things (IoT), robotics has become a part of industrial development. The power sector is also adopting IoT to improve sustainability and have better efficiency [[10](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_010)]. Due to the low cost involved with sensor, actuator purchasing issues, IoT is becoming popular day by day. The IoT theory concept connects wired or wireless nodes to a particular machine’s sensors and equipment on a shared network. In general, to reduce related threats with wired networks, IoT-based wireless systems are commonly picked. Bearing in mind the near future needs, where any computer is smart, digital, and Internet-connected. For more information, the authors examined some technical details that relate to IoT supporting technologies, protocols and applications in [[6](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_006)]. IoT technology and other new developments have been explained, such as block chain, big data analytics, and cloud computing. Ankit and Suresh [[11](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_011)] developed a remote monitoring system of solar PV using IoT. They mainly focused on the cost issue using the GPRS module and microcontroller. Shrihariprasath [[12](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_012)] proposed a model for monitoring solar PV power generating unit using the IoT concept. Besides solar cell temperature monitoring, IoT systems can be the right solution for fault diagnosis for solar panels and other accessories. Chagitha Ranhotigamage and Subhas Chandra Mukhopadhyay [[13](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_013)] developed a model to acquire faulty PV array for well-time dover hauling purposes.

Even from renewable sources, the power supply must be predicted in the electricity grid because the capacity and quality variations can negatively impact the entire grid’s physical health and the superiority of lifecycle of its consumers. When the number of green energy production plants multiplies, optimum sizes, locations and installations would still need to be determined. Additionally, the operation of the intelligent grid, which integrates renewable energy plants, is a challenge [[14](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_014), [15](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_015), [16](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_016)]. In [[17](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_017)], researchers develop cost-effective, expendable green energy for hybrid solar plants combining various systems with thermal energy storage (TES). The energy storage integration allows for a variable solar generation to be dispatched and reduced by combining multiple generation technologies. However, a carefully balance between financial and technological efficiency is essential for designing efficient and cost-effective hybrid power plants. Bahaidarah [[18](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_018)] developed a hybrid system consisting of a solar collector and PV cell with back surface water cooling. The schematic overview of the experimental design is shown in [Figure 9.1](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_001). The setup is made with 230 W capacity monocrystalline type PV module. The backside of the module is equipped with a cooling system. A water pump with a power of 0.5 hp and 3.65 liter/min discharge was used to circulate water in the system. An increase in 9% efficiency was achieved in this system.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_001.jpg)

Figure 9.1: Schematic diagram of the PV (photovoltaic) unit with back surface water cooling [[18](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_018)].

Effective fault diagnosis of PV solar modules has a significant impact on efficiency improvement. It is required to understand the behavior of the solar module’s various operating parameters in different environmental conditions for successful fault diagnosis purposes. Different types of faults that occurred in PV arrays are mentioned in [Figure 9.2](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_002). The PV module’s health status was monitored through continuous monitoring of data from different points in the system. Based on these historical data, the machine learning (ML) model was developed, as shown in [Figure 9.3](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_003) [[19](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_019)]. In this chapter, authors have demonstrated that the performance of ML model is greatly depends on sessional effect. Especially, in the winter season, the trained model performance is greatly affected.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_002.png)

Figure 9.2: Classification of different types of faults in PV array [[19](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_019)].

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_003.jpg)

Figure 9.3: Architecture of fault diagnosis system of PV module [[19](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_019)].

This chapter presents intelligent solar tracking, state-of-the-art cooling, ML-based thermal mapping with intelligent health condition monitoring of solar panel, and IoT-based intelligent green energy-harvesting techniques.

## 9.2 World energy scenario

A significant indicator of global primary energy production is the generation of electricity [[28](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_028), [29](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_029), [30](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_030), [31](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_031), [32](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_032), [33](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_033)]. In the production of electricity, a considerable portion of the world’s main forms of energy (coal, natural gas, gasoline and biomass) is burned up. During the transformation process for thermal power plants, above 50% of the energy input dissipates as a loss. [Figure 9.4](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_004) shows the world power production capacity from 2008 to 2018 prepared by the REN21 community [[20](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_020)]. It is observed that the global renewable power generating capacity is increasing day by day. Still, the world mostly depends on nonrenewable sources of energy.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_004.jpg)

Figure 9.4: World power production capacity, by energy source, 2008–2018 [[20](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_020)].

### 9.2.1 Renewable energy scenario in the world

Solar energy is ahead of all alternative sources of energy in terms of its output potential. From 2012 to 2018, annual green energy addition is observed in [Figure 9.5](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_005) to be highest from solar energy and minimal from bio-power and geothermal energy sources. Renewable electricity yielding to develop at a healthy speed in the field of power generation in 2018.

Internationally, an estimated 181 GW was deployed, marginally more than in 2017, and overall installed capacity increased by more than 8% [[20](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_020)]. The pace of new capacity adds its level during the year after years of steady expansion, and then worldwide green energy production capacity amounted to approximately 2,379 gigawatts by the expiration of 2018\. One hundred and eighty-three solar PV with approximately 100 GW were added once again. During 2018, the worldwide composition of green power infrastructure installed began to change. At the end of the year, hydroelectric power rarely accounted for 50% of the overall capacity of green electricity in use, slipping below 48 %. Around the same time, wind power grew to about 25% of the output potential of renewable energy, while solar energy, for the first time, hit 20%. Overall, renewable energy has increased to account for more than one-third of the world’s total generation capacity.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_005.jpg)

Figure 9.5: Yearly added energy from renewable source by technology, by 2012–2018 [[20](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_020)].

### 9.2.2 Energy scenario of solar PV in the world

[Figure 9.6](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_006) shows the world production capacity of solar energy using PV cells. From the figure, it is observed that maximum solar energy is produced by China. China and Japan are pretty close to each other. The most crucial point is that the global approach to the development of solar power is very positive. Above 500 GW has been produced in 2018, as depicted in the figure.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_006.jpg)

## 9.3 Concept of IoT

IoT denotes a system of inter-connected devices through Internet that can be used to receive and transfer data avowing any human interference through a wireless network. There are more wired computers in our world than humans. The IoT will change the way the rest of the wired planet communicates with corporations, states and individuals. IoT is a current trending concept that foresees a bright prospect in which ordinary stuff will be fitted with microcontrollers, digital communication receivers, and good protocol stacks to interact with each other and with users integral part of the Internet [[21](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_021)]. Therefore, the IoT idea seeks to make the Internet ever more immersive and ubiquitous. In addition, by enabling simple accessing technique and contact with an extensive range of objects, such as small household accessories, CCTV cameras, tracking devices, actuators, screens, electric vehicles, and so on, the IoT will encourage the production of many applications, exploiting the potentially massive amount and variability of data produced by these products to provide people with various innovative amenities. This model is also applicable in numerous diverse sectors, such as home automation, plant automation, medical assistance, mobile healthcare, elderly assistance, intelligent power and smart grid regulation, automotive, traffic control, and many others [[22](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_022)]. [Figure 9.7 d](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_007)isplays the main IoT applications in different fields. The total number of smart devices connected together is expected to hit up to 75 billion by 2025, according to Statista [[23](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_023)] as demonstrated in [Figure 9.8](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_008).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_007.jpg)

Figure 9.7: Application of IoT.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_008.jpg)

Figure 9.8: Prospects of IoT [[10](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_010)].

## 9.4 Intelligent system for renewable energy (RE) technologies

There is an almost bewildering preference for networking solutions for programmers and hardware engineers working on computers and IoT applications. Many networking tools are well known, such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, ZigBee, and cellular 2 G/3 G/4 G. Some of the most used technologies are explained in the following [sections.](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_s_009_s_004_s_001)

### 9.4.1 Bluetooth-based RE system

Bluetooth is considered a wireless technology benchmark used to relay data between fixed and mobile devices and create personal area networks using ultra-high-frequency radio waves in different radio bands, ranging from 2,402 to 2,480 GHz. It was initially adopted as a cellular alternative to RS-2322 data cables. Due to low power consumption and low-cost involvement, it was a popular technology. However, short-area coverage is a limitation of this technology. Some essential features of a Bluetooth-based system are as follows [[24](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_024)]:

1.  Technology: Principal arrangement Bluetooth 4.2

2.  Bandwidth frequency: 2.4 GHz (ISM)

3.  Distance covered: Around 50–150 m

4.  Data transfer range: Up to 1 Mbps

### 9.4.2 Wi-Fi-based RE system

Wi-Fi networking is often ideal for many developers, particularly given the widespread Wi-Fi inside LANs in the home setting. Wi-Fi generally transmits data in the form of the simple packet to various access points in the system. Due to its sizeable current infrastructure and quick data transmission and the capacity to accommodate huge volumes of data, it takes no further clarification about the Wi-Fi-based RE system’s usability. Some essential features of a Wi-Fi-based system are as follows [[24](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_024)]:

1.  Technology: Based on 802.11 n

2.  Bandwidth frequencies: 2.4–5 GHz bands

3.  Distance covered: Around 50 m

4.  Data transfer range: Maximum 600 Mbps; however, the normal range is 150–200 Mbps.

### 9.4.3 GSM-based RE system

One of the emerging technologies of the century is based on GSM, or Global System for Mobile Communications. It deals with the architecture of a stand-alone embedded device that can remotely track and monitor several devices independent of distance constraints. The term embraced in the embedded domain is commonly sending and receiving SMS. Some essential features of a GSM-based system are as follows [[24](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_024)]:

1.  Technology: Global System for Mobile Communications (GSM)/High-Speed Packet Access (HSPA)

2.  Bandwidth frequencies: 900 to 2,100 MHz

3.  Distance covered: Maximum distance covered is 35 km for GSM; 200 km for HSPA

4.  Data transfer range: 35–170 kbps for General Packet Radio Service (GPRS), 120–384 kbps Enhanced Data for Global Evolution (EDGE), 384 kbps to 2 Mbps Universal Mobile Telecommunications Service (UMTS), 600 kbps to 10 Mbps High Speed Packet Access (HSPA)

## 9.5 IoT-based efficient solar PV system

We are passing the era of the fourth industrial revolution. Nowadays, technology has become even more hybridized. For the present and future generations, this hybridization is a gift. A blending of existing ideas with new technology is the proposed paradigm. With a conventional solar power generation method, some additional parameters have been added to improve the performance of the proposed solar panel model.

The proposed efficient PV model is demonstrated in [Figure 9.9](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_009). To preserve the surface temperature of solar cell, an automated water cooling system was introduced. The operational decision of the cooling system is made by an intelligent operating system based on IoT. There was no conventional thermal sensor installed on the PV surface in the proposed system. To have proper temperature mapping, a drone-operated thermal camera is used here. An ML algorithm is used here to identify the exact boundary of the solar panel to extract accurate data. When the PV solar panel’s surface temperature reaches a predefined reference point, the cooling device begins operating automatically. If the required surface temperature is reached, the cooling mechanism is deactivated. In addition to the cooling system, a solar tracking system is also used to maximize the benefits of solar irradiation. This monitoring is also done by IoT automatically.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_009.jpg)

Figure 9.9: Line diagram of the proposed efficient PV model with major components.

### 9.5.1 Solar tracking

The energy digs out from the solar PV module depends on solar irradiation. The solar collector panel should still be natural to the incident radiation for optimum energy production. The solar sun trackers shift the solar collector to follow the sun’s direction and maintain the solar collector’s orientation at an ideal tilt angle. The solar monitoring system significantly increases the PV panel’s energy efficiency. The following criteria can be used to classify solar monitoring systems as shown in [Figure 9.10](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_010).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_010.jpg)

Figure 9.10: Type of solar tracking system.

The solar tracker can be subdivided into active and passive systems depending on the moving mechanism, as shown in [Figure 9.11](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_011). Passive tracking devices use the pressure variance of exceptional fluids with a low boiling point from memory-formed material to change the tracking unit’s axes. The pressure difference is generated by the shaded and illuminated sides of the monitoring device’s thermal differences. Until the pressure difference is balanced, the monitoring system travels, making it easier to extend and watch in austere conditions. Passive machines are used very seldom and do not need auxiliary power supplies to operate. On the other side, an active solar tracking device consists of electromechanical components like microcontroller, electrical motor, sensor, actuator, and gearbox for its operation.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_011.jpg)

Figure 9.11: Solar tracking system: (a) passive and (b) active [[3](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_003)].

[Figure 9.12](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_012) shows the open-loop solar tracking system. The open-loop control system uses an accurate algorithm to determine the point of the PV tracking system. The modules’ position can be accurately determined; as the sun’s relative position is accurately measured using the relationships between the sun and the earth for any location on earth. The algorithm is loaded into the microprocessor and, without using suggestions to assess outcomes, is based on date and time control. A closed-loop control system is much more popular than an open loop, as depicted in [Figure 9.13](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_013). In this system, a sensor is used to obtain the tracker position. The closed-loop control system is better for effective control of the solar tracking system. Yet, it has some drawbacks. Feedback signal also creates noise in the control system that enhances stability issues. In addition to this extra expense for the sensor, it often absorbs more power than the open-loop control system.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_012.jpg)

Figure 9.12: Open-loop solar tracking system.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_013.jpg)

Figure 9.13: Closed-loop solar tracking system.

### 9.5.2 Intelligent temperature monitoring system

The optimal power energy extraction from the solar PV depends on the surface temperature of the module. When illuminated with 1 kW/m² of luminous power, the PV module’s power is one of these parameters when the PV cell’s temperature is 25 °C. A PV module’s performance is greatly hampered if it crosses a certain threshold value. The thermographic analysis technique has been adopted in the proposed model to obtain the panel’s accurate thermal data [[25](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_025)]. The developed system solves some of the issues associated with the application of the contact sensor and provides accurate readings and greater flexibility at the same time. It has been done by using a drone-operated thermal camera. Due to the wide-area coverage of a drone camera, it is essential to have proper thermal mapping. This is done with the help of an appropriate ML algorithm. By monitoring various PV module performance parameters, this ML technique also helps identify various faults in the system.

### 9.5.3 Impact of temperature on the efficiency of PV cell

Analyzing the overall performance of the PV cell, it can be inferred that cell temperature is one of the critical elements of energy conversion process of solar PV [[28](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_028), [29](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_029), [30](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_030), [31](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_031), [32](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_032), [33](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_033)]. The effect of temperature on PV cell performance can be estimated through the use of following equations [[4](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_004), [26](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_026)]:

(9.1) $P_{SP} = P_{{SP}\eta} \ast f_{SP}\left( \frac{G_{T}}{G_{T{.STC}}} \right) \ast \left\lbrack {1 + \beta_{r}\left( {T_{sp} - P_{{SP}{.STC}}} \right)} \right\rbrack$(9.2)*η*[ele] = *η*[T.STC][*γ* * log[10]*G*[T] + [1 − *β*[r](*T*[sp] − *P*[STC])])

where *P*[*S**P**η*] denotes the nominal output power of the solar panel. *η*[*e**l**e*] is the electrical efficiency of PV module at standardized testing conditions (STC) of 25 °C. *G*[*T*] is used for mean solar radiation received by the PV cell in hour basis, while *G*[*T*.*S**T**C*] indicates the solar irradiation estimated at STC.

The derating factor is denoted by *f*[*S**P*] in %. The actual temperature of the solar PV cell is denoted by *T*[*s**p*].

### 9.5.4 Cooling system

The cooling mechanism helps preserve the temperature at the desired limit of a structure or unit, or machinery. To work safely, every device has a safe temperature limit. The machine may fail past this limit, or productivity may decrease. Several cooling techniques are used to increase the performance of the machine. They are discussed below.

#### 9.5.4.1 Water cooling

One of the most effective cooling methods available in the thermal industry is water cooling. It is used widely because of its affordability, nontoxic behavior, and high heat removal rate. For the cooling of solar PV by the water cooling process, two methods are adopted: using a water jacket and spraying water directly on the PV panel. The use of a water jacket is more beneficial than spraying water directly. A water cooling system of PV cell is shown in [Figure 9.14](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_014).

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_014.jpg)

Figure 9.14: Cross-sectional view of water cooling system of solar cell [[18](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_018)].

Sainthiya [[27](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_027)] developed a model to improve PV modules’ efficiency using the front surface cooling method for summer and winter seasons. The efficiency obtained in that paper was around 27% in both conditions. The experimental setup has been shown in [Figure 9.15](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_015). They used a 75 W polycrystalline type PV module with a cross-sectional area (110 cm × 65.6 cm). A positive displacement pump with a capacity of 33 L/min was used for circulating water in the PV module.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_015.jpg)

Figure 9.15: Schematic diagram of a complete water cooling system of PV cell [[27](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_ref_027)].

#### 9.5.4.2 Water cooling with additives

Because of its scale formation property, the efficiency of water cooling is reduced to some degree. Water sedimentation induces water channel clogging. Decreasing thermal efficiency is the ultimate consequence. Water accelerates the degradation of metal parts and is a suitable biological growth source. To solve this problem, the inclusion of additives plays a crucial role. They also assist in improving the pace of water heat reduction.

#### 9.5.4.3 Air cooling

Air cooling is a less effective method compared to water cooling. With air cooling strategies, two forms of cooling are generally possible: forced convection and natural convection. Due to the gradient of pressure, normal convection takes place. It needs no external supply of energy at all. But the process of cooling is sluggish. Force convection happens as the device is supplied with pressurized air from an external fan or blower. Due to the faster cooling rate, it has more efficiency.

## 9.6 Salient features/importance of IoT-based system

IoT has become one of the most trending technological fields that can convert any traditional electronic home appliance or product into a smart one. It has kept and still keeping a vital role in controlling the hi-tech machinery from a remote place. Due to its flexibility and following salient features, researchers and industrial people are adopting IoT.

1.  Easy access of all vital plant’s or system’s data from a distant location at a low cost.

2.  Convenient data transferring.

3.  Helpful for analyzing big data.

4.  Real-time data analyzing facility from a remote location.

5.  Faster decision-making.

## 9.7 Experiment with solar tracking system

### 9.7.1 Experimental setup

The experiment was performed in an active solar tracking system, where the experimental configuration consists of a PC, PLC, solar transmission and a multimeter, as shown in [Figure 9.16](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_016), and the photograph of the setup is shown in [Figure 9.17](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_017). The sensor installed identifies variations in light intensity as the path of the sun changes. The error signal produced by the sensor is used to detect the position of the solar panel. The sensor performance and software logic will force the solar panel to adapt accordingly with the help of a motor, rack and pinion mechanism, in the direction of a high intensity of sunlight.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_016.png)

Figure 9.16: Schematic diagram of the experimental setup.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_017.jpg)

Figure 9.17: Photographs of a PLC-based solar tracker.

The solar tracker is designed for two-stepping motors, a rack, a pinion and a light sensor. A flexible stand supports the solar panel. The pinion rotates the stepper from both ends of the panel so that the panel will rotate with the aid of the rack in a required angle.

### 9.7.2 Results

Experiments have been conducted with solar tracking using PLC-based controller. The power output from the solar PV cell with and without tracking is presented in [Figure 9.18](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_018). From the figure, it can be seen that using a solar tracking system, the power generation capacity of the solar PV has been increased to some extent. Maximum power has been produced by the system is almost 4.4 W, whereas without solar tracking system this power has been fallen into 3.5 W. The calculated efficiency improvement is estimated to be 28.52%.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_018.jpg)

Figure 9.18: Power generationversus daytime curve.

[Figures 9.19](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_019) and [9.20](#9783110713794_025550.xhtml_b_9783110714043-009_fig_020) show the simulation results of the solar PV module with and without a water cooling system and with and without solar tracking. The time vs. power output curves show a significant improvement by introducing water cooling and solar tracking.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_019.jpg)

Figure 9.19: Power generationversus daytime curve with and without water cooling.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-009_fig_020.jpg)

Figure 9.20: Power generationversus daytime curve with and without solar tracking.

## 9.8 Conclusions

An intelligent solar energy-harvesting technique based on IoT has been presented in this chapter. The current global energy situation and approaches to green energy development have been discussed early in this chapter. To better interpret IoT implementations, the IoT principle with associated communication technologies has also been briefly clarified. Afterward, different cooling methods, solar tracking and monitoring systems have also been described that enhance the efficiency of solar power generation. The proposed model can be an excellent solution to meet the global energy crisis. Some experimental and simulation results have also been included using water cooling systemand solar tracker. The result obtained from this experiment and simulation looks promising, especially the 28.52% improvement using solar tracking systems.

# Editor’s Brief Biographies

![](graphic/converted/b_9783110714043-010_ingr_001.png) **Dr. Neeraj Priyadarshi** received M. Tech. degree in Power Electronics and Drives in 2010 from Vellore Institute of Technology (VIT), Vellore, India, and PhD from Government College of Technology and Engineering, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India. Dr. Priyadarshi has Post Doc from Bioenergy and Green Engineering, Department of Energy Technology, Aalborg University, Esbjerg 6700, Denmark and CTIF Global Capsule (CGC), Department of Business Development and Technology, Aarhus University, Birk Centerpark 40, 7400, Herning, Denmark .In previous postings he was with BITT Ranchi, MIT Purnea, JK University, Geetanjali Institute, Global Institute and SS Group, Rajasthan, India.His current research interests include power electronics, Control system, power quality, and solar power generation. Currently he is associated with Bioenergy and Green Engineering, Department of Energy Technology, Aalborg University, Esbjerg 6700, Denmark. He has published over 60 papers in Journals and conferences of high repute like IEEE Systems Journal, IET Electric Power Applications, IET Power Electronics IEEE Access Journal, Electric Power Components and Systems, International transaction of Electrical Energy System, Wiley Energies MDPI, International Journal of Renewable Energy Research etc and granted 10 International patents. He successfully organized two international workshops and one National workshop in different organization. He is also invited as a Guest speaker in many International/National conferences. Dr. Priyadarshi is a reviewer of IEEE Transactions Industrial Informatics, IEEE Transactions Industry Applications, IEEE Systems Journal, Journal of Cleaner Production, Elsevier, International Journal of Electrical Power and Energy Systems, IET Power Electronics, Energy for Sustainable Development, Elsevier, Energy Reports Journal, Elsevier, Sustainable Energy Technologies and Assessments, Elsevier, International Journal of Energy Research, Willey, Electronics Letters, IET, Electric Power Components and Systems, Taylor and Francis, IEEE Access, IET Renewable Power Generation, International Journal of Modeling and Simulation, International Journal of Renewable Energy Research, International Journal of Power Electronics and Drives and Reputed Elsevier SCI indexed Journals etc.Under him guidance 25 DST, Government of Rajasthan projects have been sanctioned for financial support. Dr. Priyadarshi is working as a chief editor of four edited books of renowned publisher (Intelligent Renewable Energy Systems: Integrating Artificial Intelligence Techniques and Optimization Algorithms, Wiley and Advances in Power Systems and Energy Management, Springer, and De Gruyter Publisher, Germany).

Scopus URL: [https://www.scopus.com/authid/detail.uri?authorId=57195679733](https://www.scopus.com/authid/detail.uri?authorId%3D57195679733)(Scopus Author ID: 57195679733)

Google Scholar URL: [https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=G_YrT-oAAAAJ&hl=en](https://scholar.google.com/citations?user%3DG_YrT-oAAAAJ%26hl%3Den)
